*Delirium* has long been recognized as a complication of cardiac surgery. Postoperative delirium is estimated to occur in 20% to 25% of cardiac surgery patients. It is more common among older adult patients and those with greater comorbid disease burden. Delirium is triggered by multiple potential causes, and in the cardiac surgery patient, it is the product of a baseline vulnerability, stress from the surgical intervention and hemodynamic perturbations, and iatrogenesis in the postoperative phase of care. Cardiac surgery procedures are being performed on older patients with recent coronary syndromes, higher NYHA classifications, lower left ventricular ejection fractions, cardiogenic shock, and frailty (defined as an accumulation of deficits resulting in a vulnerability to stressors)—all of which are associated with increased rates of postoperative delirium. Postoperative delirium is variable in duration, with most episodes lasting only a few days; however, in up to 20% of individuals, delirium may persist for weeks or months.

The importance of delirium was previously dismissed, as it was believed to be a transient and benign entity. More recently, increasing attention has been paid to delirium's negative effects on postoperative outcomes, including long-term survival, hospital readmissions, and cognitive and functional recovery. Despite its strong association with adverse postoperative events, delirium is often underrecognized as a source of end-organ dysfunction.

**Risk Factors.** Patient-related factors being examined for their association with postoperative neurocognitive difficulties include silent cerebral infarction, cerebral autoregulation, alterations in blood-brain barrier function, and brain protein biomarkers such S100Beta, glial fibrillary acidic protein, tau, neurofilament light, and neuron-specific enolase. Rudolph and colleagues identified four risk factors associated with an increased risk for postoperative delirium after cardiac surgery: previous stroke or transient ischemic attack, low Mini Mental State Examination score, abnormal serum albumin, and a Geriatric Depression Scale score indicating depression. Other researchers have further identified elevated preoperative EuroSCORE, older age (≥70 years), number of comorbidities, history of delirium, alcohol use, type of surgery, blood transfusion, mechanical ventilation, postoperative low cardiac output, inadequate management or overtreatment of pain, sleep deprivation, use of benzodiazepines, and infection as additional risk factors.

**Management.** Postoperative delirium can be difficult to manage. Thus, prevention and management should begin in the preoperative phase of care by identifying key risk factors for its occurrence in the postoperative phase. Risk should be documented in the patient's chart, and delirium risk should be communicated to the intraoperative team during the OR huddle and at handover to the ICU team postoperatively.

In the postoperative phase, a systematic delirium screening tool that assesses the full spectrum of delirium presentation (hypoactive, hyperactive, or mixed) should be incorporated as part of routine monitoring in the ICU at least once per nursing shift. When an active screening program in the ICU is lacking, delirium goes undiagnosed in more than 70% of cases. A systematic methodology for delirium screening in the cardiothoracic ICU will use either a confusion assessment method or an intensive care delirium screening checklist. Delirium screening should be undertaken in tandem with arousal assessment. The Riker Sedation Agitation Scale and the Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale are some of the more widely used tools for determining levels of sedation and agitation.

Once delirium is detected, it is important to determine the underlying cause (e.g., pain, hypoxemia, low cardiac output, sepsis) and initiate appropriate treatment. Nonpharmacologic strategies are the first-line component of management and should include the multicomponent Society of Critical Care Medicine ICU "ABCDEF Liberation Bundle" in the postoperative cardiac surgery ICU (see: https://www.sccm.org/ICULiberation/Home). A multicenter randomized trial examining a multicomponent family-support intervention found that the intervention was associated with higher ratings of the quality of communication and a shorter ICU LOS. Although these data were not specific to the cardiac surgery patient, it is reasonable for the perioperative team to consider incorporating this approach within their delirium management protocols.

Pharmacologic strategies using prophylactic medicines such as clonidine or antipsychotics have been found to reduce delirium rates, as shown in several RCTs. Current recommendations indicate that the use of antipsychotic medications should be limited to patients who have particularly distressing symptoms or who pose safety risks to themselves or the healthcare team. An exception may be dexmedetomidine, a selective α₂-adrenergic agonist that may facilitate lighter sedation, shorten the duration of mechanical ventilation, reduce the risk for delirium, and provide analgesia in critically ill patient populations. However, dexmedetomidine is also associated with bradycardia and lowered blood pressure. In a recent rapid practice guideline, the writing committee stated that the use of dexmedetomidine could be considered "...if the desirable effects including a reduction in delirium are valued over the undesirable effects, including an increase in hypotension and bradycardia...."

Importantly, McPherson and colleagues reported that the use of chemical restraints (such as benzodiazepines) or physical restraints and restraining devices predisposed patients to a greater risk for delirium, pointing to potential areas for quality improvement in the cardiac surgical ICU.

<details class="med-details"><summary>

##### TABLE 4.19: Perioperative Neurocognitive Disorders</summary><div class="details-content">

| Time Period | Term and Definition | Comments |
|---|---|---|
| Preoperative | Mild NCD<br>Major NCD | As in community |
| | **Perioperative Cognitive Disorders** | |
| Emergence from anesthesia | Emergence excitation or delirium | |
| From: Immediately postoperative<br>Until: Expected recovery (between 30 days and 3 months)* | Delirium (postoperative**)<br>Delayed Neurocognitive Recovery# | The time for expected resolution is based on perioperative conditions (e.g., complications/infection/prolonged hospitalization) |
| From: Expected recovery (30 days to 3 months)<br>Until: 12 months | Mild NCD postoperative<br>Major NCD postoperative | "Postoperative" is an indicator of the temporal association with the anesthesia/surgery event |
| Beyond 12 months | Mild NCD<br>Major NCD | As in community if a new diagnosis after this time |

* The time point from at least 30 days when the effects of anaesthesia and surgery should have resolved.
** The postoperative specifier will be applied where delirium is persistent beyond hospital discharge.
##### Also applies to assessments prior to medical readiness for discharge, although it should be noted any decline prior to readiness for discharge is unlikely to represent a delay in recovery.
Adapted with permission from Evered L, Silbert B, Knopman DS, et al. Recommendations for the Nomenclature of Cognitive Change Associated with Anaesthesia and Surgery-2018. *Anaesthesiology*. 2018;129(5):872-879

*NCD,* neurocognitive disorder.

</div></details>

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Mood Dysfunction</summary><div class="details-content">

The patient's preoperative mood characteristics play a major role in postoperative mood state. Contemporary studies, however, estimate the rates of preoperative depression to be 10% to 30% in patients generally and 15% to 47% in cardiac surgery patients, and it is associated with new postoperative delirium and longer-term mood and cognitive issues.

**Risk Factors.** The absence of daily access to an empathetic individual who is not part of the surgical team, both before and after surgery, is an important risk factor for increased anxiety and depression postoperatively. Postoperative depression after cardiac surgery is an independent risk factor for subsequent cardiac events and is associated with increased risk for hospital readmission and death. Postoperative depression is probably influenced by a complex interaction between genetic and psychosocial predisposing factors, by neuroendocrine activation, and by the release of proinflammatory factors.

**Management and Treatment.** The most important methods for preventing mood dysfunction include daily access to family or caregivers and physician attention to the patient as a sensitive and threatened human being. When the mood state becomes severely abnormal, psychiatric assistance is required. Lastly, appropriate communication to the patient's primary caregiver at the time of hospital discharge should include follow-up of both cardiac rehabilitation and mental health needs.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Other Neurologic Issues</summary><div class="details-content">

**Visual Defects.** Visual-field defects are sometimes perceived by the patient and confirmed by testing; these usually regress or disappear spontaneously. Difficulty focusing, such as is required for reading, is sometimes experienced in the early postoperative period (up to 30 days). These issues can be classified as mild-delay neurocognitive recovery and often resolve without specific treatment.

**Seizures.** Postoperative seizures are relatively uncommon after cardiac surgery but can have important consequences for the patient. Incidence rates of clinically evident seizures range from 0.5% to 8.0%. Postoperative seizures usually appear within the first 2 days after surgery and are typically short (2–3 minutes), but they can deteriorate to status epilepticus. When seizures do occur, recurrence rates of 40% to 66% have been reported.

Goldstone and colleagues, in an analysis of approximately 2600 patients undergoing cardiac surgery, determined that 71% of observed seizures were of the generalized tonic-clonic type, 26% were simple/complex partial seizures, and 3% were status epilepticus. The lowest seizure rates were for isolated CABG, and higher rates were observed for combined valve/CABG and aortic procedures. Postoperative seizure was associated with a longer ICU and hospital LOS and nearly fivefold higher operative mortality (29% vs. 6% in patients with no seizure). Reported causes include ischemic strokes (the most common etiology in this cohort), DHCA, aortic calcification or atheroma, older age (>75 years old), previous strokes, critical preoperative state, cerebral air embolism, medication toxicity or withdrawal, substance (alcohol) withdrawal, and other operative drugs such as larger doses of tranexamic acid.

Immediate management should focus on the "ABCs" of acute resuscitation: airway, breathing (with an increase in Fio₂), and circulation. If the seizure has not self-terminated, IV benzodiazepine is the appropriate first-line agent. If this is unsuccessful, one of several antiseizure medications (e.g., valproic acid, levetiracetam, phenytoin) can be given. If seizures do not terminate with one antiseizure medication load, the perioperative team should next infuse propofol or midazolam and plan to secure the airway with endotracheal intubation.

Once the patient is stabilized, appropriate neurologic imaging (CT) should be undertaken to rule out acute ischemic stroke or hemorrhage. An electroencephalogram may be required to rule out nonclonic seizures in patients with remaining somnolence or delirium. The patient's medical history should be queried for a previous history of seizures or other conditions that may predispose the patient to seizure activity (e.g., substance withdrawal). Decisions about the need for longer-term antiepileptics should be made in conjunction with a neurology consultation.

</div></details>

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
##### Gastrointestinal Subsystem</summary><div class="details-content">

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Nutrition</summary><div class="details-content">

It has long been understood that a patient's nutritional status before cardiac surgery is an important driver of clinical outcomes. Well-nourished patients have better outcomes and lower postoperative mortality after cardiac surgery. That said, malnutrition, defined as an unintentional imbalance in nutritional intake (not necessarily decreased intake), is evident in 20% of patients before cardiac surgery. As the overall population ages and patients undergoing cardiac surgery are generally more frail, identifying and reversing malnutrition have become increasingly important for the perioperative team.

The typical perioperative fasting, inflammatory, and regenerative processes (such as wound healing, immune function, and acute-phase reactants) induce an exponential increase in nutritional demand that results in a demand-supply imbalance (i.e., a state of metabolic stress). Traditional practices such as fasting before surgery (typically for 6–12 hours) and delaying or inadequately initiating postoperative nutrition (typically for 2 days because of complications like swallowing difficulties or postoperative nausea and vomiting) further compound issues with malnutrition. In addition, the metabolic and immune response to injury induces insulin resistance, further aggravating a calorie-deficient state. Ultimately, this perioperative demand-supply imbalance produces an extensive insult to a reserve-deficient physiologic system (as in a typical frail patient), disproportionately diminishing the patient's health status and hindering recovery to baseline functional capacity.

To address this vulnerability, an expert consensus panel on enhancing recovery after surgery has recommended the following management strategies.

- 1. *At baseline:* The perioperative team should determine the patient's baseline frailty, nutritional status (using a dedicated screening tool and a serum albumin), and glycemic control (by measuring hemoglobin A1C). *Rationale:* To establish baseline risk and identify possible targets for therapeutic intervention and optimization.
- 2. *Preoperatively:* Clear liquids should be continued until 2 to 4 hours before surgery, and solid foods until 6 hours before surgery; carbohydrate loading (24 g of a complex carbohydrate beverage) should be initiated 2 hours before surgery. *Rationale:* Preoperative intake of a complex carbohydrate load is associated with reduced insulin resistance and tissue glycosylation, improved postoperative glucose control, and enhanced return of gastrointestinal function; it does not appear to be associated with increased risk for aspiration.
- 3. *Preoperatively:* When possible, oral nutritional supplementation should be used in malnourished patients for 7 to 10 days before cardiac surgery. *Rationale:* Evidence from the noncardiac surgery literature suggests that supplementation with whey protein provides essential branched-chain amino acids, notably leucine, and is superior to other protein sources for stimulating muscle protein synthesis and attenuating muscle loss in older adults.

In critically ill patients for whom oral intake cannot be initiated in a timely fashion, particularly those with an anticipated long ICU LOS, early enteral nutrition or combined enteral and parenteral nutrition has been recommended. Nonetheless, recent data from both critical care and cardiac surgery patients suggest that the perioperative team should exercise caution when using early feeds with mineral supplementation, given the lack of proven benefit, risk for bowel ischemia (particularly in patients taking multiple vasopressors), and greater risk for renal dysfunction. Decisions about the timing, route, and dosage of early postoperative feeds in a critically ill patient who has undergone cardiac surgery should be made on an individual basis, in conjunction with the interdisciplinary team.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Postoperative Nausea and Vomiting</summary><div class="details-content">

Postoperative nausea and vomiting (PONV) is one of the most frequently reported complications after anesthesia and surgery. Despite advances in anesthesia practice and antiemetic therapies, the overall incidence of PONV has remained relatively unchanged over the past 4 decades. Whereas the incidence of PONV is 20% to 30% in the noncardiac surgery population, PONV rates in the cardiac surgery population are reportedly as high as 70%.

Current guidelines recommend using a preoperative risk score to identify a patient's propensity for PONV and considering prophylaxis with ondansetron, betamethasone plus droperidol, or perhaps aromatherapy in high-risk patients. Using a nasogastric tube after cardiac surgery has not been found to consistently reduce PONV. A large Cochrane review reported an association between postoperative chewing gum use and quicker return of gastrointestinal function; however, this approach has not been studied extensively in cardiac surgery patients specifically. Therefore, extrapolating current guidelines to the cardiac surgery patient should be undertaken with consideration of any potential proarrhythmogenic side effects of the agents being administered.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Gastrointestinal Complications</summary><div class="details-content">

Acute dysfunction of the gastrointestinal subsystem is generally uncommon but has been reported in 0.3% to 4.5% of patients who undergo cardiac surgery. Significant abdominal complications, including deep sternal wound infections, renal failure requiring dialysis, and multisystem organ failure, can be devastating. As a result, these complications are associated with high morbidity and mortality rates that can approach 50%. Key in management is early identification and rapid confirmatory diagnosis by the perioperative team, followed by definitive therapy to improve outcomes in these patients.

The most prevalent abdominal complications include ileus (most common), gastrointestinal bleeding and ulcerations (next most common), mesenteric ischemia and perforated viscus (most lethal), cholecystitis, pancreatitis, and hepatic dysfunction or failure. The risk factors associated with gastrointestinal complications include: age >65 years, advanced congestive heart failure (NYHA classification III/IV), endocarditis, COPD, peripheral vascular disease, low serum albumin, complex surgery (reflected in longer CPB and cross-clamp times), postoperative low cardiac output syndrome, hemodynamic instability on admission, prolonged need for inotropic or vasopressor support, use of an IABP, end-stage renal disease requiring hemodialysis, and acute hepatic dysfunction.

*Ileus.* Postoperative ileus is a temporary lack of normal intestinal muscle contraction that occurs when gas in the gastrointestinal tract produces abdominal distention, generally within 24 to 48 hours of surgery. Bowel sounds can be heard initially. Although not a component of normal recovery, this complication is usually benign and subsides without specific treatment. However, when severe, postoperative ileus can be associated with significant morbidity, including prolonged hospital LOS and mortality.

Key mechanisms of postoperative ileus include fluid overload, use of opioids for analgesia, neurohormonal dysfunction, and gastrointestinal stretching and inflammation. If untreated, a severe colonic pseudo-obstruction can lead to cecal overdistension and possible perforation, which usually carries a high risk of death. The critical cecal diameter at which perforation is more likely ranges from 9 to 12 cm.

Management of postoperative ileus begins with prevention. Enhanced recovery guidelines include limiting preoperative fasting, using neuraxial and multimodal analgesia, avoiding fluid overload, removing urinary catheters early after surgery to promote mobilization, and using coffee and chewing gum to promote the return of gastrointestinal function and to facilitate recovery.

Should postoperative ileus develop, conservative measures (which include maintaining preventive strategies) and the use of neostigmine remain the mainstay of treatment and are sufficient in most cases. Colonic decompression or surgical intervention is not usually required, unless the ileus is severe or the bowel has perforated. A newer agent, alvimopan, is a peripherally acting μ-opioid receptor antagonist recommended for postoperative ileus in the enhanced recovery guidelines for noncardiac surgery. However, its utility and safety in the cardiac surgery setting have not been well studied to date.

*Gastrointestinal Bleeding.* Gastrointestinal bleeding after open cardiac operations usually comes from the upper part of the gastrointestinal tract (where the pathology is typically hemorrhagic gastritis or duodenitis) and less commonly from the lower tract. Patients who are mechanically ventilated or at high risk for gastric or duodenal stress ulcers and not on enteral nutrition should receive proton pump inhibitor prophylaxis (in preference to H₂ histamine blockers) to minimize the risk for gastrointestinal bleeding.

Postoperative gastrointestinal bleeding is more common after valve procedures than after CABG. A possibly related factor is a preoperative history of AF and anticoagulation with warfarin. Further, combined anticoagulation/antiplatelet therapy, intermittent episodes of hypoperfusion, and use of vasoactive medications (which can cause splanchnic vasoconstriction) have been associated with gastrointestinal bleeding after cardiac surgery. Additional risk factors include age ≥70 years, ejection fraction <35%, congestive heart failure, cerebrovascular disease, chronic kidney disease, and gastrointestinal disease. Although these conditions can be managed by the perioperative team with effective resuscitation and endoscopic therapies and with CT angiogram-directed coil embolization, mortality remains high, ranging from 9% to 38%.

Bleeding from the lower gastrointestinal tract, although less common than upper gastrointestinal bleeding, can be serious, with a reported mortality rate of 17%. The most common sources of bleeding include diverticula and vascular malformations, colitis, neoplasia, and anorectal bleeding. In addition, 10% of lower gastrointestinal bleeding has traditionally been presumed to have an upper gastrointestinal source, and hemodynamically unstable patients with hematochezia may first require upper endoscopy. Current American College of Gastroenterology guidelines suggest that, in hemodynamically stable patients, appropriate bowel preparation followed by colonoscopy should be the initial diagnostic procedure and should be performed within 24 hours. Radiographic interventions (tagged RBC scintigraphy, CT angiography, angiography) should be considered in high-risk patients with ongoing bleeding who do not respond adequately to resuscitation and who are unlikely to tolerate bowel preparation and colonoscopy. Importantly, although the American College of Gastroenterology generally recommends against the use of NSAIDs, patients with high-risk cardiovascular disease and lower gastrointestinal bleeding are advised to continue any ongoing aspirin therapy (secondary prophylaxis). The decision to maintain or discontinue dual antiplatelet therapy should be based on individual patient assessment by the interdisciplinary team.

*Mesenteric Ischemia and Bowel Perforation.* Intestinal necrosis develops uncommonly but insidiously, particularly in sedated patients after cardiac operations. The estimated incidence rate ranges from 0.06% to 0.20%. Initially, intestinal necrosis may not be readily apparent until gastrointestinal metabolic acidosis (lactate) develops, signs of sepsis or septic shock appear, or the inevitable perforation in the necrotic area causes pneumoperitoneum. Diagnostic delay can occur as a result of this latency, such that mortality associated with postoperative ischemic bowel is high, ranging from 46% to 75%. Risk factors include the presence of peripheral vascular disease, hemodynamic instability or vasoplegia requiring high-dose vasopressors, and low cardiac output.

The perioperative team must have a high index of suspicion for bowel ischemia, particularly in patients who develop an intolerance to enteral nutrition, clinical signs of shock (with or without an elevated lactate), and abdominal pain or distention. Initial management consists of IV fluid administration, antimicrobial therapy, and correction of hypoperfusion (if possible). Rapid investigation includes an abdominal radiograph, a CT scan with contrast, and/or endoscopy.

In all instances, early diagnosis and surgical intervention remain the only effective means of reducing mortality from bowel ischemia after cardiac surgery. Bowel perforation secondary to a duodenal ulcer additionally requires an initial infusion of proton pump inhibitors and assessment for *Helicobacter pylori*. Nonocclusive mesenteric ischemia induced by postoperative low cardiac output can be difficult to diagnose, as the typical CT scan findings (portal venous gas, pneumatosis intestinalis, "string of sausages," abnormal contrast-induced bowel wall enhancement) are poorly sensitive. Thus, endoscopy or surgical exploration may be required for diagnosis.

*Acute Cholecystitis.* Two large retrospective series found that the rate of acute cholecystitis after cardiac surgery ranges from 0.03% to 0.08%, with acalculous cholecystitis being more common than calculous cholecystitis. When cholecystitis develops after cardiac surgery, it produces the same clinical syndrome it causes in other patients: severe pain, leukocytosis, and right upper quadrant tenderness. The precise mechanism underlying acalculous cholecystitis is not well understood, but hypoperfusion of the gallbladder, bile stasis, and inflammatory mediators have been proposed. Liver function tests often display the usual abnormalities seen with cholestasis. Workup consists of ultrasonography or CT, depending on physician preference.

Rady and colleagues found that presentation for acute cholecystitis can be quite delayed (a median 26 days after the cardiac surgery procedure). Perhaps because of this delay, mortality among patients with this complication is high, ranging from 15% to 42%. Patients with acute cholecystitis but without overt peritonitis should initially be treated conservatively with appropriate antibiotics. However, a lack of significant improvement within 48 hours or a worsening clinical picture should lead to placement of a percutaneous cholecystostomy drainage tube and/or surgical intervention.

*Acute Pancreatitis.* Pancreatitis early following cardiac surgery was first described in 1976. Some evidence of pancreatic cellular injury is present in about 25% of patients after open cardiac surgery, typically presenting with hyperamylasemia, increased serum lipase, or both. In most cases, these increased levels gradually decline, with few, if any, clinical sequelae. Occasionally (in <5% of patients with elevated amylase), severe pancreatitis develops, along with pancreatic abscess or hemorrhagic pancreatitis, and this condition is associated with high rates of mortality.

Current guidelines recommend that, in addition to establishing a diagnosis on the basis of clinical symptoms and laboratory testing, additional contrast-enhanced CT and/or MRI of the pancreas should be obtained in patients for whom the diagnosis is unclear or who do not improve clinically. Patients with acute pancreatitis and concurrent acute cholangitis should be evaluated as to whether endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography is needed. Resuscitation with appropriate fluid administration should be done within the first 12 to 24 hours if clinically indicated. Routine use of prophylactic antibiotics is not recommended, even in patients with severe acute pancreatitis or sterile necrosis. Conversely, in patients with infected necrosis, appropriate antibiotics may be useful in delaying intervention.

In milder presentations, oral feeding can be started immediately if the patient is extubated and has no nausea and vomiting. In more severe presentations, enteral nutrition is recommended over parenteral feeding to prevent infectious complications; in fact, parenteral nutrition should be avoided. In stable patients with infected necrosis, any surgical, radiologic, or endoscopic drainage should be delayed for 4 weeks, when possible, to allow a wall to develop around the necrosis.

*Liver Dysfunction.* Descriptions of hyperbilirubinemia (serum bilirubin concentration of 3–4 mg/dL) after cardiac surgery date back to the early years of the field. Hyperbilirubinemia develops in 10% to 40% of patients after cardiac surgery. The most common preoperative risk factor is venous congestion due to heart failure. Postoperative jaundice is largely secondary to an increase in conjugated bilirubin and has been associated with prolonged CPB and cross-clamp times, the use of IABP, the administration of inotropes, and the number of blood and plasma transfusions. More recent reports have indicated that hyperbilirubinemia, although often benign, is associated with adverse patient outcomes such as prolonged ICU stay, new-onset infection, low-output syndrome, greater need for invasive ventilation and renal replacement therapy, and higher mortality rates (ranging from 4%–17%).

Frank hepatic necrosis may develop when cardiac output is acutely and severely reduced. In patients with fulminant liver failure (<0.4%), mortality can exceed 90%. A recent large meta-analysis examining the severity of preexisting liver dysfunction (identified by using the Model for End-stage Liver Disease score preoperatively and/or the Child-Turcotte-Pugh score after cardiac surgery) found that liver dysfunction in patients undergoing cardiac surgery was associated with a three- to fivefold higher risk for short- and long-term mortality. In addition, an increase in other perioperative adverse events, such as neurologic complications, prolonged ventilation, CSA-AKI, sepsis, and bleeding, was observed.

Farag and colleagues suggested that the timing and level of peak bilirubin concentration may help differentiate patients with an underlying liver function pathology due to systemic hypoperfusion. Specifically, delayed and steep hyperbilirubinemia (median 3.5 postoperative days with an optimal bilirubin cutoff value of 5.35 mg/dL) was linked to hypoperfusion and poor outcomes.

Managing a patient with hyperbilirubinemia and liver dysfunction requires the perioperative team to ensure adequate cardiac flow, optimize volume and other organ status, and supply adequate nutritional support.

*Watery Diarrhea and Clostridium Difficile Infection.* As a complication of cardiac surgery, watery diarrhea may accompany or follow abdominal distention or appear de novo toward the end of the first week. This can be secondary to electrolyte replacement, enteral feeding, and medications. The perioperative team should be alerted if the diarrhea becomes more frequent, explosive, or if it is accompanied by mucus and/or blood, particularly if associated with leukocytosis. Discontinuing oral antibiotics should be considered when diarrhea develops. Diarrhea can also be caused by ischemia of the bowel secondary to a long period of low cardiac output, and this ischemia may lead to small or large bowel infarction (see earlier), or it may be a form of ulcerative colitis or proctitis. Diagnosis can be made by using contrast CT or endoscopy. If the bowel ischemia and ulceration progress, laparotomy and bowel resection are required but even with such treatment, prognosis is poor.

A common, important, and potentially serious cause of watery diarrhea after cardiac surgery is *Clostridium difficile*, which colonizes the intestinal tract after the normal gut flora have been altered by broad-spectrum antibiotics. The prevalence of this complication ranges from 0.7% to 5.4% (although the higher rate may be due to colonization rather than true infection). Risk factors for development of *C. difficile*–associated diarrhea include advanced age, greater cumulative antibiotic days, longer ICU stay, blood product transfusion, and longer time on a ventilator. *C. difficile* is associated with increased mortality when severe (i.e., antibiotic-associated pseudomembranous colitis).

Recent guidelines have classified *C. difficile* infections as mild to moderate (mild abdominal pain and cramps that may progress to altered electrolyte balances and dehydration) and severe (mild-moderate symptoms with fever, leukocytosis, and hypoalbuminemia). Progression to fulminant *C. difficile* infection (often heralded by the absence of diarrhea) is associated with higher mortality rates due to the development of toxic megacolon, bowel perforation, septic shock, and eventual multiorgan dysfunction. Lastly, *C. difficile* infections may recur in 10% to 13% of cases and in as many as 60% of patients after one or two recurrences.

The current guideline-recommended management bundle for *C. difficile* infection includes:

- Infection control with antibiotic stewardship, discontinuation of unnecessary proton pump inhibitors, hand hygiene, isolation measures, and environmental disinfection.
- Contact (enteric) precautions with *C. difficile* carriers.
- Testing of diarrheal stools from at-risk patients with clinically significant diarrhea (≥3 loose stools in 24 hours) and no obvious alternative explanation. In patients with ileus, polymerase chain reaction testing of perirectal swabs is an alternative approach.
- CT imaging for patients with clinical manifestations of severe or complicated *C. difficile* colitis. Ultrasonography or flexible sigmoidoscopy may be useful in critically ill patients suspected to have pseudomembranous colitis and who cannot be transported to the CT scanner.
- Avoidance of empirical therapy for *C. difficile* infection unless there is a strong suspicion of *C. difficile* infection. Oral metronidazole should be limited to treating an initial episode of mild to moderate *C. difficile* infection, with oral vancomycin reserved for patients who do not respond to metronidazole. Repeated or prolonged courses of metronidazole should be avoided due to risk of cumulative and potentially irreversible neurotoxicity. Either oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin is recommended for treatment of all patients with severe *C. difficile* infections.
- Early surgical consultation for all patients with *C. difficile* infection who progress to sepsis or septic shock.

Information on the use of probiotics is limited, other than that they could be useful as an adjunct to antibiotics for recurrent *C. difficile* infection; however, this requires additional study in cardiac surgery patients. Consultation with infection control or an infectious disease specialist, particularly in cases of recurrent *C. difficile* infection, should be undertaken per institutional protocols.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Summary Points</summary><div class="details-content">

- Well-nourished patients have better outcomes and lower postoperative mortality after cardiac surgery. Developing a perioperative strategy to identify nutritional deficiencies, manage PONV, and establish nutrition postoperatively are important considerations for the perioperative team.
- Mild gastrointestinal complications after cardiac surgery are relatively common. However, significant complications such as gastrointestinal bleeding or mesenteric ischemia, although less common, are associated with a significant risk of death and require prompt identification and aggressive management.

</div></details>

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
##### Endocrine Subsystem</summary><div class="details-content">

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Glycemic Control</summary><div class="details-content">

Hyperglycemia (in both the OR and ICU) is associated with adverse outcomes after cardiac surgery. The prevalence of DM among cardiac surgery patients varies from 7% to 40%. Thus, screening for suboptimal glycemic control or DM is recommended for all patients undergoing cardiac surgery. Glycated hemoglobin (HbA1C) is a useful clinical measure for quantifying long-term glucometabolic state. Clinical practice guidelines recommend a target HbA1C <7% in patients with DM; however, approximately 25% of patients undergoing cardiac surgery have suboptimal glycemic control (HbA1C >7%), and 10% of these patients have undiagnosed DM. In patients undergoing CABG or valvular procedures, higher HbA1C is a strong predictor of mortality and morbidity, hospital LOS, and long-term survival, irrespective of previous DM status, and a perioperative HbA1C level >7% is associated with increased risk for postoperative complications (acute renal failure, cerebrovascular accident, postoperative MI, sternal wound infection) in patients undergoing CABG. A serum glucose level >200 mg/dL is associated with an increased risk for death and adverse events, regardless of whether or not DM is present.

Due in part to seminal work by Van den Berghe, increased attention is being given to appropriate glycemic management in critically ill patients. In a large population undergoing cardiac surgery, the investigators studied the utility of continuous insulin infusion for tightly regulating blood glucose levels at 80–110 mg/dL. The study found a significant reduction in overall in-hospital mortality, mechanical ventilation time, number of blood transfusions, bloodstream infections, acute renal failure requiring dialysis, and other morbidities. Further work by Lazar and colleagues further showed that tight glycemic control (using a combination of glucose, insulin, and potassium) in patients with DM undergoing cardiac surgery was associated with improved survival, fewer ischemic events, and fewer wound complications. Nonetheless, more recent clinical trials of tight glycemic control have not replicated these benefits (or have shown an increase in mortality), and concerns have been raised about the potential downside of hypoglycemia.

More recently, a tailored glycemic control strategy was developed that takes into account a patient's baseline HbA1C. This process, referred to as "time in band," seeks to reduce relative hypoglycemia for a given patient based on the adequacy of baseline glycemic control. In other words, those with suboptimal glycemic control (HbA1C >8%) are permitted to maintain a higher perioperative serum glucose level. Relative hypoglycemia has been associated with higher rates of mortality in the critical care population; however, this has not been extensively studied in postoperative cardiac surgery patients.

As a result, current recommendations pertaining to perioperative glycemic control include the following:

**Preoperatively**
- Perform routine HbA1C screening for risk stratification.
- Seek to achieve a blood glucose level of 120 to 180 mg/dL (6.7–10.0 mmol/L) while the patient awaits surgery; consider using short-acting subcutaneous insulin, with blood glucose checks every 4 hours.

**Intraoperatively**
- In non-DM patients, consider using a small (5 IU) IV bolus of insulin if the blood glucose level is >180 mg/dL (>10.0 mmol/L), as well as hourly checks.
- In non-DM patients, consider using a continuous IV insulin infusion to maintain blood glucose of 150 to 180 mg/dL (8.3–10.0 mmol/L) during surgery if blood glucose is persistently >180 mg/dL (>10.0 mmol/L).
- In patients with DM, start a continuous IV insulin infusion at the beginning of surgery and continue throughout to maintain a blood glucose level >150 (>8.3 mmol/L) and <180 mg/dL (<10.0 mmol/L).

**In the ICU**
- In patients with or without DM, use a continuous insulin infusion if the blood glucose level is >180 mg/dL (>10.0 mmol/L) for a target of 150 to 180 mg/dL (8.3–10.0 mmol/L) during the ICU stay; this should be accompanied by hourly blood glucose monitoring, which can be deescalated to every 4 hours if the patient is stable.

**Post-ICU**
- Consider initiating a combination of short-acting and long-acting subcutaneous insulin at 50% of the total previous 24-hour insulin dose (in the ICU) and then titrate by checking the blood glucose level every 4 hours to a target of 150 to 180 mg/dL (8.3–10.0 mmol/L).
- Consider restarting oral anti-diabetes medications at 50% of the preoperative dose when the patient is on oral feeding.

**At Hospital Discharge**
- In patients with preexisting or newly identified DM, consult with an endocrinologist or DM specialist before the patient is discharged.
- For all patients with DM and those who have started a new glycemic control regimen, provide inpatient education regarding glucose monitoring and medication administration and communicate appropriately with the patient's primary care provider.

Sodium-glucose co-transporter-2 (SGLT2) inhibitors and short-acting glucagon-like peptide 1 receptor agonists are newer classes of medications that are being used with increased frequency due to their glycemic control, improved outcomes in patients undergoing CABG, and their impact on heart failure and renal dysfunction. Although a detailed review of these agents is beyond of the scope of this chapter, the perioperative team should be aware of several key issues.

- Neither of these medication classes is associated with hypoglycemia, but they can potentiate hypoglycemia when combined with a sulfonylurea or insulin.
- Some SGLT2 inhibitors are associated with genital infection and, less commonly, euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis when precipitated by stress. Therefore, SGLT2 inhibitors should be held before surgery (per local institutional guidelines) and avoided in patients with type 1 DM.
- Because SGLT2 inhibitors have been associated with hypovolemia-related side effects (osmotic diuresis), they should be held in patients with a systolic blood pressure <100 mmHg.
- Generally, SGLT2 inhibitors are not recommended if the estimated GFR is <45 to 60 mL/min/1.73 m² due to a decrease in glycemic efficacy.
- Reinitiating these agents in the postoperative period remains an area for further study.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Summary Points</summary><div class="details-content">

- Estimates suggest that 40% of patients undergoing CABG have DM, and >10% have undiagnosed DM at the time of surgery and increased morbidity and mortality compared with non-DM patients. Glycemic control in the perioperative period reduces morbidity and mortality.
- Glycemic control with a continuous insulin infusion and a blood glucose goal of 140 to 180 mg/dL confers the most benefit. The perioperative team should be aware of current guidelines on the optimal preoperative, intraoperative, and postoperative glycemic management of patients with and without DM.
- Both SGLT2 inhibitors and glucagon-like peptide 1 receptor agonists should be held before surgery.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Adrenal Insufficiency</summary><div class="details-content">

Acute adrenal insufficiency after cardiac surgery was first described by Alford and colleagues in 1979. In that review of 4364 patients, 5 (0.1%) had symptoms consistent with acute adrenal insufficiency. Although the etiology was not well understood, in some of the reported cases, hypotension was preceded by hypertension, lending to the notion that the subsequent adrenal insufficiency was secondary to a hemorrhagic infarction of the adrenal gland. It was thought that this might have been precipitated by the use of CPB with anticoagulant therapy, an added risk. Symptoms generally appear between postoperative days 4 and 10 and consist of abdominal and flank pain, abdominal distention, altered mental status, fever, and occasionally shock.

Assessing for adrenal insufficiency should include ruling out other potential causes of shock and using biochemical analysis to confirm the diagnosis. A cosyntropin stimulation test can provide credence to the diagnosis if the serum cortisol level remains low 60 minutes after administration of a 250-mg dose. Confirmatory evidence (e.g., a CT or MRI scan) of acute adrenal insufficiency should be obtained. Initial treatment includes prompt glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid replacement, use of vasopressors, and fluid resuscitation. Subsequent lifetime oral treatment is indicated.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Thyroid Dysfunction</summary><div class="details-content">

A reduction in plasma free triiodothyronine (T₃), but not thyroxine (T₄), can occur early after cardiac surgery and remain low for at least 24 hours. Classic work by Timek and colleagues found that exogenous T₃ may improve the amplitude of myocardial fiber shortening by accelerating Ca²⁺ transients in an in vivo preparation of catecholamine stimulation. There is some clinical evidence that exogenous T₃ (0.6–0.8 µg/kg) may improve cardiac output immediately after CABG in patients with depressed ejection fraction. RCTs examining the utility of T₃ administration have found an association with an increase in cardiac output but no consistent improvement in postoperative outcomes. Thus, routine T₃ administration is not current practice.

</div></details>

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
##### Hematologic Subsystem</summary><div class="details-content">

In cardiac surgical patients, managing hematologic function is a critical component of perioperative care. As a result, a team-based approach to PBM is essential to contemporary practice. This ranges from managing anticoagulation preoperatively to monitoring coagulation intraoperatively to managing bleeding postoperatively. Specific considerations for postoperative bleeding are as follows.

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Postoperative Bleeding</summary><div class="details-content">

Although the reasons for bleeding after CPB and heparin reversal are multifactorial, a surgical (mechanical) cause should always be considered. Procedures that confer a high risk for excessive bleeding include reoperation, complex procedures with long CPB times, and aortic surgery. Multiple factors contribute to coagulopathy after CPB and cardiac surgery, including preoperative anticoagulation use, reoperations, time and duration of CPB, and pharmacologic strategies, including antifibrinolytic agents.

The reported rates of surgical reexploration for bleeding range from 2% to 8%, depending on institutional criteria and case mix. Moulton and colleagues identified patient older age, preoperative renal insufficiency, non-CABG procedures, and prolonged CPB time as independent risk factors for reexploration, which was associated with increased operative mortality and morbidity.

Inflammatory responses are associated with CPB for various reasons—for example, nonendothelial surface interactions that cause contact activation and proinflammatory responses (including fibrinolysis and platelet and neutrophil activation). Platelet abnormalities, both quantitative and qualitative, universally develop during and after CPB, as does coagulation upregulation as a result of dilution, activation, and consumption of critical hemostatic factors. During CPB, hypothermia, mechanical injury, and consumption of platelets impair platelet function and are important contributors to coagulation defects. Despite adequate rewarming after CPB-related hypothermia, hemostatic dysfunction can persist.

Transfusion of hemostatic factors, factor concentrates, and platelets is the mainstay therapy for managing bleeding, as is the use of bleeding-management algorithms, usually with viscoelastic testing and the use of intraoperative hemostatic checklists. Transfusing allogeneic blood products should be limited or avoided whenever possible for several reasons, for example, higher risk for death, infection, and other adverse events, although patient-related risk factors could also cause these outcomes. Heparin rebound can occur after initial reversal, in which case additional protamine can be administered, usually as an infusion of ~25 mg/h for several hours.

Protocols and institutional practices vary, with different transfusion thresholds. Unfortunately, most patient blood-management practices focus on RBCs. In critically ill, actively bleeding, or in those with unstable hemodynamics, higher thresholds may be warranted. Comparative studies examining RBC/hemoglobin thresholds did not report the physiologic implications of different hemoglobin concentrations.

In addition to traditionally used allogeneic blood products, several coagulation factor concentrates and procoagulants have been added to the perioperative teams' armamentarium, including fibrinogen concentrates, factor 8 inhibiting bypass activity, recombinant activated factor VIIa, fibrinogen concentrates, and prothrombin complex concentrates. These evolving therapies are effective in managing coagulopathic bleeding from cardiac surgery and may reduce the need for surgical reexploration.

When excessive bleeding continues after a reoperation in which no specific bleeding point is identified, prompt hematologic consultation and specific treatment are indicated. It is tempting to regress to the indiscriminate, costly, and sometimes hazardous use of FFP and platelet concentrates and additional doses of protamine. Despite the common use of FFP, no data support its efficacy, and FFP can only correct INR to ~1.5. Optimally, specific management protocols include (1) repeated measurements of ACT and administration of additional protamine only when indicated; (2) consideration and possibly measurement of excessive fibrinolysis, along with use of tranexamic acid, although this is best used prophylactically; and (3) consideration of possible platelet dysfunction, depletion, or both, and administration of platelet concentrates. Platelet transfusions continue to be empirical, as we cannot monitor platelet function, and most algorithms use platelet numbers for transfusions. When warfarin reversal is urgently needed, prothrombin complex concentrates should be administered according to current guidelines. Fibrinogen is an important target in bleeding patients, in whom fibrinogen levels <200 mg/dL should be treated with cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrates, depending on their availability.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Chest Tubes</summary><div class="details-content">

Chest drainage tubes must be properly placed in the OR for them to function well in the early postoperative period and keep the pericardial and pleural spaces free of blood. When the patient is being transported to the ICU, chest tubes are connected to an underwater seal. Once the patient is in the unit, the tubes drain into an initially empty container in which a negative pressure of 10 to 20 cm H₂O is continuously maintained. Routine "stripping" of the chest tubes by nursing personnel is unnecessary and potentially dangerous, and the technique should be used only for clearing clots. Provisions are made for continuous precise measurement of drainage from the chest tubes; this can be incorporated into an automated care system.

When chest drainage is excessive, prompt reoperation for bleeding is indicated. The surgical and perioperative team should develop specific institutional quantitative guidelines for communication on threshold to discuss concerns about excessive chest tube output, with or without hemodynamic instability. Although reoperation for bleeding is not risk-free, relatively early reoperation for bleeding usually stops excessive bleeding, even when no specific bleeding points are found. This approach may avoid pericardial tamponade, minimize the administration of allogeneic blood, and ease the management of all subsystems.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Summary Points</summary><div class="details-content">

- The surgical and perioperative teams should conjointly develop an approach to PBM for postoperative bleeding that includes a mechanism for effective communication and an algorithmic approach to blood product and procoagulant agent use.

</div></details>

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
##### Special Considerations after Cardiac Surgery</summary><div class="details-content">

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Acid-Base Balance</summary><div class="details-content">

As indicated earlier, some *metabolic acidosis* (base deficit ≥2 mEq/L) may be present during the early hours after cardiac surgery, even when the patient is convalescing normally. It is left untreated if arterial pH is ≥7.4 and Paco₂ is ≥30 mmHg. When Paco₂ is <30 mmHg, the base deficit is treated before adjusting Paco₂ appropriately upward. When pH is <7.4, the base deficit is treated. However, if convalescence is otherwise normal, treatment is delayed for 4 to 8 hours in adults and for 2 to 4 hours in infants, by which time the base deficit may have cleared spontaneously. In infants, particularly, it is best to avoid an additional sodium load whenever possible.

A mild *metabolic alkalosis* may be present 24 hours after operation in normally convalescing patients, probably related in part to the citrate load contained in the anticoagulant solution of banked blood. Metabolic alkalosis is self-correcting under these circumstances and is not treated.

When large amounts of homologous blood containing sodium citrate have been transfused, important metabolic alkalosis can occur. Metabolism of the citrate leaves bicarbonate as the only anion available to balance sodium ions. This situation may be exacerbated after 2 to 4 days by hydrogen ion migration from the cells because potassium ions replace hydrogen ions, and serum potassium falls, sometimes precipitously, in association with potassium loss in urine. Relatively large amounts of potassium chloride must be given under such circumstances (up to 200 mEq in 24 hours), but the dose should be titrated against 4-hourly serum potassium measurements. The alkalosis is associated with a fall in serum chloride and a compensatory rise in Paco₂. Provided renal function is adequate, the situation will correct itself. If it continues to worsen, the carbonic anhydrase inhibitor acetazolamide is administered, usually intravenously at a dose of 5 mg/kg up to 250 mg in adults once or twice a day as needed until the alkalosis is corrected.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Cardiac Tamponade</summary><div class="details-content">

In an echocardiographic study, Weitzman and colleagues found that 103 of 122 consecutive patients (84%; CL 80%–88%) had pericardial effusions during recovery from cardiac surgery; Ikäheimo and colleagues found a similar prevalence. *Large* pericardial effusions 4 to 10 days postoperatively develop in 30% of patients and are more common when early postoperative bleeding is excessive or after heart transplant. Effusions reach their maximum size in most patients around postoperative day 10, generally regressing spontaneously thereafter. Many smaller pericardial effusions are asymptomatic and require no treatment.

**Detection and Treatment**

Cardiac tamponade develops in only about 1% of patients with pericardial fluid. Recognizing and treating acute tamponade early after cardiac surgery can be challenging (see also "Acute Cardiac Tamponade" and "Treating Low Cardiac Output" in Section I). A chest radiograph usually shows enlargement of the cardiac silhouette before symptoms appear; nonetheless, tamponade may not become apparent until as late as 4 months after operation.

Cardiac tamponade occasionally develops in the ICU, in which case prompt reoperation is required (see "Acute Cardiac Tamponade" in Section I). Delayed cardiac tamponade may develop several days to several weeks after the patient leaves the OR. Although delayed tamponade is uncommon, it is more common among patients being treated with anticoagulants and possibly when smaller incisions are used. If unrecognized, delayed cardiac tamponade can cause serious morbidity and mortality. Clinical signs and symptoms may be subtle, but one or more of the following are usually present: progressive and unexplained weakness and lethargy; progressive dyspnea on exertion or orthopnea; unexplained hepatic congestion or hepatomegaly, ascites, or peripheral edema; elevated jugular venous pressure; pulsus paradoxus; widening of the cardiac silhouette on chest radiography; and unexplained prerenal dysfunction.

Treatment consists of pericardial decompression by either pericardiocentesis, preferred whenever possible, or surgical drainage. In one review of 21,416 cardiac surgical patients, 327 (1.5%) had postoperative pericardial effusion, which was evacuated by echocardiography-guided pericardiocentesis (n = 169, 52%) or surgical drainage (n = 75, 23%); the remaining patients were managed conservatively. With surgical drainage, the subxyphoid portion of the incision is reopened, and the pericardial space is entered at the diaphragm. If a week or more has elapsed from surgery, great care is required to gently dissect along the diaphragmatic surface until the fluid collection site is entered. Undue haste can result in accidental entry into the right ventricle, which can be friable and soft. Mortality has occurred under such circumstances.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Summary Points</summary><div class="details-content">

- The signs and symptoms of delayed tamponade can be subtle.
- The perioperative team should have a high index of suspicion when there is an unexplained failure to progress and widened mediastinum on chest x-ray, particularly in patients on anticoagulation.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Infection</summary><div class="details-content">

Infection and wound complications, although uncommon after cardiac surgery, are associated with high rates of morbidity and mortality. The many people involved and the length and complexity of the operations make bacterial contamination more likely in cardiac surgery than it is in shorter and simpler procedures. Control of the surgical and ICU environments and awareness among personnel of the possibility of contamination significantly reduce the prevalence of infection. Meticulous aseptic technique by the surgical, anesthetic, and perfusionist teams is an essential part of prophylaxis against infection in cardiac operations.

**Prevention**

Infection within or around the heart, particularly when prostheses have been used, is a major threat to the patient's life. Therefore, preventative measures should be employed, such as appropriate nutritional support in patients who are malnourished, body weight control in obese patients, smoking cessation, perioperative glycemic control, and pulmonary physiotherapy (particularly in smokers and patients with COPD). Prophylactic antibiotic therapy is recommended for all cardiac operations, as is *Staphylococcus aureus* decolonization with intranasal mupirocin ointment and chlorhexidine bath, with appropriate skin preparation in the OR. Hair removal (if necessary) can be done by using an appropriate clipper immediately before the operation; shaving is strongly discouraged, as it has been shown to increase the risk for microinjuries and bacterial growth on the patient's skin.

The Joint Commission (www.jointcommission.org), initially founded in 1951, has set forth a number of national quality core measures, including the Surgical Care Improvement Project, which presents specific recommendations for perioperative antibiotic prophylaxis for patients undergoing cardiac surgery. Antibiotic levels in the blood must be adequate immediately before and during the operation and while any intravascular or endotracheal device is in place. The perioperative team should engage in tracking compliance with their chosen surgical site infection prophylaxis approach.

Typically, skin flora consists of *Staphylococcus* and *Streptococcus* species and will be covered by a first-generation or second-generation cephalosporin. The current Surgical Care Improvement Project recommends the use of cefazolin 2 gm (3 gm for patients weighing >120 kg) for this purpose, to be given 1 hour before the skin incision. If a patient has a known beta-lactam allergy or requires additional gram-negative coverage with a fluoroquinolone, such as vancomycin, this should be administered 2 hours before the skin incision due to the infusion time. In patients with methicillin-resistant *S. aureus*, vancomycin is given preferentially; however, data from a meta-analysis suggest that both cefazolin and vancomycin should be given in this context to ensure adequate coverage of both methicillin-sensitive and methicillin-resistant *S. aureus*. Intraoperative repeat doses (every 3–4 hours) may be required for longer operations. Current guidelines recommend that antibiotic prophylaxis be continued for up to 48 hours. Coverage beyond 48 hours is not recommended, even in patients with chest tubes, lines, or other drains, as it is not associated with benefits and may increase the risk for *C. difficile* infection and the development of antimicrobial-resistant pathogens. Evidence supporting the efficacy of additional topical antibiotics, implantable gentamicin-collagen sponges, or irrigation is inconsistent at best, and high-quality RCTs will be required to establish benefit from these approaches.

The perioperative team should seek to minimize the risk of infection by removing intravascular lines, endotracheal devices, and urinary catheters as early as possible postoperatively. Infection can also be minimized through simplified postoperative care, early transfer out of the ICU, early ambulation and oral alimentation, and early hospital discharge. Negative-pressure wound therapy has been suggested as a modality for reducing surgical-site infections in patients at higher risk for postoperative wound complications. A large RCT examining surgical-site infection rates with mono- or dual-antibiotic prophylaxis in combination with conventional wound dressing versus negative-pressure wound therapy is currently recruiting (Prevention of Infections in Cardiac Surgery [PICS] Prevena® Study; NCT: 03402945).

**Body Temperature**

Variation in body temperature is common and can persist for 2 weeks or longer after surgery (Fig. 4.17). During this period, routine orders for repeated blood, sputum, and urine cultures and other special studies, when there is no indication other than fever, typically have low yield and increase healthcare expenditures. Using antibiotics empirically in this context should be avoided, given the risks mentioned earlier for *C. difficile* infection and antimicrobial-resistant and opportunistic pathogens.

By contrast, severe and increasing hyperthermia during the first 6 to 36 hours after surgery is a potentially lethal complication that should prompt the team to investigate and treat for malignant hyperthermia, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, or serotonin syndrome (particularly in cases with recent methylene blue or selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor use).

**Surgical Site and Sternal Wound Infection**

Sternotomy closure after cardiac surgery is most commonly done by using wire cerclage, which reopposes the cut edges of the sternum with a wire or band wrapped around or through the two portions of bone; the wire or band is tightened to pull the two parts together. Alternatively, sternal plating (i.e., rigid sternal fixation) has been used on the premise that it reduces side-to-side movement (which might not be achieved with wire cerclage) and has been associated with less postoperative pain, quicker mobility progression, improved quality of life, and fewer sternal complications (i.e., mediastinitis and nonunion), thereby offsetting the additional cost of the rigid fixation plates within 6 months of surgery. Surgeons may opt for this closure method in patients at higher risk for sternal complications, such as those with a high body mass index, previous chest wall radiation, severe COPD, or steroid use.

The sternal wound and graft-harvesting locations are the most common sites of infection. Healthcare-associated infections, such as central line-associated bloodstream infections, catheter-associated urinary tract infections, and ventilator-associated infections, are the most common source of sepsis and provide key metrics for evaluating perioperative team performance. Quality-improvement initiatives for these events are well described and should be regularly reviewed and audited by the perioperative team as part of ongoing performance improvement initiatives.

*Sternal Wound Complications.* Sternal wound complications can be classified as superficial or deep and as early or late. Superficial sternal wound infections are limited to the pectoralis fascia and above (i.e., the skin and subcutaneous tissue), whereas deep sternal wound infections involve tissues below the fascia. Early deep sternal wound infections are more common than late infections; late infections are often a combination of superficial and deep infection.

Progression to mediastinitis, defined as an infection occurring within 1 year after surgery, is one of the most serious postoperative infections in cardiac surgery patients. The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention characterize mediastinitis as having at least one of the following: positive culture from mediastinal tissue or fluid showing bacterial organisms; evidence of mediastinitis on gross anatomic or histopathologic examination; signs or symptoms of fever (>38°C), chest pain, or sternal instability with purulent drainage from the mediastinal area; and/or mediastinal widening on imaging. A deep sternal wound infection with mediastinitis may include sternal osteomyelitis and further retrosternal, posterior mediastinal, and/or pleural space involvement.

The extent of the infection should be further characterized with CT imaging of the chest and mediastinum. Although CT evidence of retrosternal fluid (some edema or blood in the anterior mediastinum is expected in the early postoperative setting) and sternal dehiscence is not unequivocally diagnostic of infection, air pockets or sternal ostial destruction usually indicates mediastinitis.

Previous reports of mediastinitis and sternal dehiscence after bilateral internal thoracic artery–to–CABG have ranged from 0.8% to 8.0%, with mortality as high as 70%. Risk factors for mediastinitis include:

- *Preoperatively:* DM, obesity, advanced age, COPD, smoking, frailty, heart failure, female sex, renal dysfunction or failure, prolonged preoperative hospital stay, urgent or emergency surgery.
- *Intraoperatively:* use of bilateral internal mammary artery grafts (particularly in patients who are frail, malnourished, or have poor glycemic control), prolonged or complex surgery, redo operations, method of sternal closure.
- *Postoperatively:* prolonged respiratory failure, prolonged ICU LOS.

Expert consensus guidelines developed to prevent sternal wound infection have reduced estimated deep sternal wound infection rates to 0.5% to 5.0%. When sternal wound infection does occur, the associated healthcare costs, morbidity, and mortality can be very high.

*Surgical Prevention and Treatment.* In addition to the prophylaxis and prevention strategies described earlier, current surgical recommendations include use of a pedicled internal mammary artery graft and low-dose electrocautery (or harmonic scalpel) during harvest, meticulous hemostasis and chest closure, minimal use of bone wax, perioperative glycemia control, use of an external chest support vest, negative-pressure wound therapy (particularly in patients with a delayed sternal closure), and appropriate postoperative sternal precautions.

Sternal precautions are important for recovery after sternotomy; however, in a departure from traditional methods, nowadays, the early use of patient-centered mobilization protocols is recommended. The use of less-restrictive sternal precautions based on kinesiologic principles is a safe alternative that encourages movement and promotes more rapid return to normal functioning. One example of this process is "Keep Your Move in the Tube," wherein patients imagine themselves in a truncal tube, keeping their upper arms close to their bodies to allow for loadbearing activity. This produces a safe body position that allows patients to undertake previously contraindicated movements with fewer load and time restrictions. Gach and colleagues found that this approach was associated with quicker functional independence for patients and higher rates of discharge to home (versus to inpatient rehabilitation or a skilled nursing facility). Perioperative teams are encouraged to examine their practices and to use a team-based early-mobilization approach to facilitate recovery and potentially reduce the risk of surgical site infection.

When sternotomy-related superficial sternal wound infection, deep sternal wound infection, or mediastinitis occurs, the standard of care involves pain management, antibiotic therapy tailored to cultures and microbial sensitivities, and surgical debridement. The extensiveness of the surgical treatment is guided by clinical examination and CT imaging of the chest. For superficial infections, surgical incision and drainage at the surgical site can be employed (for a detailed review of surgical treatment, see van Wingerden and colleagues). Often, deep infections can be managed by debridement and daily dressing changes followed by primary closure if the tissue is healthy and free of inflammation. If the infection is extensive involving the sternum to the extent that primary wound closure is not possible, sternal wound flap closure with pectoralis, latissimus dorsi, rectus abdominis flaps, or, when needed, omental transfer can be undertaken.

In this situation, negative-pressure wound therapy can be used in the interval to permit drainage for local sepsis control, sternal stabilization, earlier extubation, mobilization or ICU rehabilitation, and wound healing and granulation. In general, complications related to negative-pressure wound therapy are minor, although a few case reports have described erosion into underlying cardiac and vascular structures. Current guidelines recommend using a barrier dressing between the gauze or foam and the heart to minimize the delivery of excessive negative pressure to the exposed, potentially friable myocardium, great vessels, or bypass conduits.

**Summary Points**

- An institutional approach to antimicrobial prophylaxis should incorporate current society recommendations, and local compliance should be audited.
- The perioperative team should seek to minimize the risk of infection by removing intravascular lines, endotracheal devices, and urinary catheters as early as possible postoperatively.
- The perioperative team should seek to use less-restrictive sternal precautions based on kinesiologic principles as a safe alternative that encourages movement and promotes more rapid return to normal functioning.

![](_page_62_Figure_7.jpeg)

**FIGURE 4.17** Proportion of patients febrile (temperature >37.8°C) after sixth postoperative day, starting with the group febrile on the sixth day. Note continuation of fever in some patients to nearly 3 weeks after operation. (From Livelli FD, Johnson RA, McEnany MT, et al. Unexplained in-hospital fever following cardiac surgery. Natural history, relationship to postpericardiotomy syndrome, and a prospective study of therapy with indomethacin versus placebo. *Circulation.* 1978;57(5):968-975.)

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Chylothorax</summary><div class="details-content">

Chylothorax, the accumulation of lymphatic fluid (chyle) in the pleural space, is uncommon (estimates of 0.3%–1.5% in cardiothoracic procedures) but morbid and potentially life-threatening after cardiac surgery. Chyle contains a high level of triglycerides in the form of chylomicrons, which gives the lymphatic fluid a milky appearance and can lead to electrolyte and nutritional deficiencies. In addition, chyle is rich in immunoglobulins and fat-soluble vitamins absorbed from the intestines. Chyle flow increases with dietary intake of fat, particularly long-chain triglycerides, which are absorbed into the lymphatic system from the intestines. The potential for relative immunodeficiency to develop during prolonged chylothorax relates to the high content of lymphocytes (primarily T lymphocytes) in chyle. Chyle accumulation in the pleural space may originate from large lymphatic vessels that have been divided or from generalized lymphatic leakage, presumably from elevated right atrial pressure.

Specifically, chylothorax is thought to result from the cutting of large tributaries of the thoracic duct (a 2–3-mm thin-walled structure that begins at the cisterna chyli at the second lumbar vertebra and enters the chest through the aortic hiatus) or, less frequently, from injury to the thoracic duct itself. This has been reported to occur after repair of coarctation of the aorta, after placement of Blalock-Taussig or (less frequently) polytetrafluoroethylene interposition shunts, after repair of PDA, and less commonly after CABG procedures.

More complex and difficult to manage is chylothorax (and on occasion, pericardial accumulations of chyle with tamponade) that develops after operations through a median sternotomy, such as atrial switch procedures for transposition of the great arteries, SVC/right pulmonary artery anastomoses, and the Fontan procedure. In these circumstances, chylothorax is thought to result from the combination of small lymph channel transections (probably in the thymus gland) and SVC pressure elevation that follows these procedures.

Chylothorax may not be immediately apparent after injury if the patient has not resumed a normal diet, and effluent may appear to be serous. A lymphangiogram demonstrating extravasation is the "gold standard" test; however, analysis of the pleural fluid revealing chylomicrons and a triglyceride concentration >110 mg/dL is generally diagnostic of chylothorax. When identified, treatment for chylothorax includes initial conservative measures (nutritional support and drainage) and surgery (thoracic duct ligation).

Initial management priorities consist of using medium-chain triglyceride formulas to ensure adequate nutrition and reducing enteral long-chain triglyceride intake to reduce chyle production. In pediatric patients, defatted human milk has been suggested. Parenteral nutrition and bowel rest may be required for patients with high-output chylothorax pleural drainage and/or as a supplemental resource for patients with malnutrition resistant to oral intake alone. The use of octreotide (100 μg every 8 hours), a synthetic polypeptide that mimics natural somatostatin by inhibiting the release of multiple intestinal enzymes, may be a useful adjunct, even for moderate to large effusions; most reports have shown benefit within 2 to 3 days of initiating treatment. The perioperative team should seek to leverage the knowledge of a dietician and a pharmacist to ensure that optimal nutritional and medication adjustments are undertaken.

In addition to nutritional support, chest tube drainage and suction of the chylothorax effusion are required. If the drainage persists for 7 days or so, doxycycline or povidone-iodine pleurodesis can be instilled through the chest tube, plus appropriate pain management can be undertaken. Surgery may be required for persistent and high-output drainage (>1000 mL/day over 5 days) despite conservative treatment. A thoracoscopic or percutaneous procedure may be the first choice, with open surgery for thoracic duct ligation reserved for those in whom less-invasive options would not be feasible or effective.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Summary Points</summary><div class="details-content">

- Chylothorax, although uncommon, can lead to significant life-threatening electrolyte, nutritional, and immunologic deficiencies.
- Initial treatment includes nutritional support with a medium-chain triglyceride oral diet, use of octreotide, and pleural chest tube drainage.
- A dietician and pharmacist should be consulted as part of the care plan.
- Percutaneous, thoracoscopic, or open surgical treatment may be required in patients with persistent high-output chylothorax despite conservative measures.

</div></details>

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
##### Interdisciplinary Approaches to Care</summary><div class="details-content">

The "heart team" model has become common in the contemporary cardiac surgery arena. This concept is based on patient-centric care that brings together various cardiac specialists (traditionally, interventional cardiologists, cardiac surgeons, imaging specialists, and the patient's general cardiologist) to evaluate optimal treatment strategies for valvular and revascularization procedures. Implementing a heart team now receives a Class I or IIa recommendation in several recent guidelines and has been incorporated into several clinical trial designs. The interdisciplinary team's integral influence on the outcomes of patients undergoing cardiac procedures is increasingly appreciated.

Through contemporary quality improvement networks, several key insights into the causes of death after cardiac procedures have been elucidated. Phase-of-care mortality analysis focuses on identifying and addressing root causes of death at the level of single seminal events (specifically, avoidable adverse events) that occur within a specific phase of care and that are found to trigger patient death. Failure-to-rescue metrics can be used to identify institutional factors responsible for poor perioperative outcomes. ERPs have the potential to standardize evidence-based perioperative care, reduce practitioner variability, and improve quality metrics and the patient experience.

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Phase-of-Care Mortality Analysis</summary><div class="details-content">

Phase-of-care mortality analysis was developed as an analytic quality improvement tool on the premise that a patient's perioperative course is an interconnected sequence of clinical events, decisions, interventions, and treatment responses. The analysis process examines five perioperative phases: preoperative, operative, in the ICU, on the ward, and during discharge planning. Careful review and analysis of these phases allow each patient's death to be categorized as avoidable or unavoidable, with an aim toward better identifying areas of care that may be amenable to mortality-reducing interventions. In seminal work by Shannon and colleagues, up to 60% of all perioperative deaths can be attributed to seminal events in the preoperative or postoperative ICU phases; similarly, other studies have found that 50% to 80% of these deaths may be attributable to avoidable adverse events during the perioperative period outside of the OR. Importantly, a significant number of preventable deaths occur in patients whose predicted risk for perioperative death was relatively low.
