The most common preoperative seminal events were related to the patient's risk-factor profile (e.g., heart failure, cardiogenic shock, myocardial viability), surgeon judgment regarding timing or the risk/benefit ratio of the surgery, and noncardiac risk factors related to patient selection (e.g., renal failure, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, cerebrovascular disease, peripheral vascular disease). The most common postoperative ICU seminal events were catastrophic events (unexpected fatal events) and perioperative team deficiencies in identifying and treating acute decompensation. Importantly, phase-of-care analysis has found that more than 40% of reviewed mortalities were "avoidable" (defined as having a >50% likelihood that the patient would have survived in the absence of the seminal event). For the perioperative team, this can provide an opportunity to target both specific death-triggering events and problematic phases of care. Several groups have made significant improvements to perioperative outcomes by addressing these avoidable terminal events.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Failure to Rescue</summary><div class="details-content">

Most deaths after cardiac surgery are not triggered by a catastrophic event resulting in sudden death but rather are secondary to the cascade mentioned earlier of clinical events, decisions, and interventions before the terminal event. Each death can, therefore, be thought of as two separate failures: failure to prevent the seminal adverse event and failure to rescue patients from the complications of these events.

Initial insight into failure to rescue was derived from a landmark study by Silber and colleagues in 1992. Defining *failure to rescue* as a death during hospitalization among patients who had a complication, the investigative team studied the relationship between adverse events and failure to rescue in nearly 6000 noncardiac surgery patients across 531 US hospitals. The key finding from this work was that adverse event rates were primarily associated with patient risk, whereas failure-to-rescue rates were primarily associated with hospital characteristics. Validation of this work in the cardiac surgery patient population has fostered advocacy for risk-adjusted failure to rescue (defined as deaths arising from one or more complications that include prolonged ventilation, stroke, reoperation, and renal failure) as a complementary metric to existing preoperative risk in the patient undergoing cardiac surgery. Including failure-to-rescue metrics aims specifically to assess the institutional effectiveness of the postoperative care team, allowing hospitals to target quality improvement efforts.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Enhanced Recovery Protocols</summary><div class="details-content">

To address the needs of an increasingly aging and frail population, a group of general surgeons in 1997 began developing a formalized, interdisciplinary process for ensuring patient readiness for surgery and promoting quicker recovery after the procedure. Specific components included optimizing perioperative nutritional status, promoting analgesia without opioids, and early postoperative feeding and mobilization. The use of such ERPs has been associated with significantly fewer perioperative complications, shorter hospital LOS, and lower costs as compared with conventional perioperative management.

Each of the surgical subspecialties has generated consensus documents providing ERP recommendations for their patients (see also www.erassociety.org). Even though standardized protocols to facilitate fast-track extubation after cardiac surgery have been commonplace for several decades, cardiac-specific perioperative ERPs are a more recent development. Cardiac surgery patients are especially vulnerable, given their increasingly advanced age, greater frailty and heightened comorbidity levels, and the likelihood of postoperative ICU admission; nonetheless, these patients have been found to benefit from ERP protocols.

Cardiac-specific ERPs should incorporate the interdisciplinary team's expertise to apply evidence-based measures that address preoperative vulnerabilities and CPB-related sequelae and incorporate best practices of care in the ICU and postoperative unit—all with the goals of optimizing the patient experience, improving outcomes, and using resources efficiently. To this end, a guidance document developed specifically for cardiac surgery perioperative teams recommends 22 potential interventions that aggregate marginal gains and synergies provided by bundling preoperative, intraoperative, and postoperative elements. The level and quality of the evidence supporting many of the ERP elements pertaining to the cardiac surgery patient are still under investigation, and an updated version of proposed recommendations is underway. It is reasonable for the interdisciplinary perioperative team to consider developing local ERPs so that elements specific to its particular setting can be incorporated.

Enhanced recovery is more than simply the implementation of a protocol. Importantly, this process requires a shift in culture, crucial interdisciplinary team building, education, planning, and processes needed to develop and sustain a successful cardiac ERP program based on best practices scaled to the clinical context of the local institution or healthcare system. In addition, patient value can be enhanced by involving a patient-engagement panel to provide additional insights for the perioperative team about ERP elements they seek to implement. Lastly, ERPs should include accountability measures so that key elements can be tracked, benchmarked, and correlated with standard and patient-centered outcomes.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Evidence-Based Perioperative Management and Team Training</summary><div class="details-content">

The perioperative care of cardiac surgery patients is interdisciplinary by design. As patients move through the five phases of care, they travel through multiple parts of the hospital and are cared for by several different teams of physicians, nurses, and allied healthcare professionals. Coordinating care across multiple disciplines and areas is of paramount importance. As discussed earlier, the use of ERPs is one method for improving the quality of care for surgical patients. Although further study in patients undergoing cardiac surgery is needed, noncardiac surgical programs have seen reductions in complication rates and mortality when ERPs are used.

One area of rescue that has garnered significant interest over the past two decades is cardiac arrest after cardiac surgery, which occurs in 0.7% to 8.0% of patients but has a high degree of reversibility, with approximately half of patients surviving to hospital discharge. A key aspect in minimizing failure-to-rescue rates after cardiac arrest is its rapid recognition and protocol-driven management of hypotensive emergencies in the postoperative ICU. To this end, a cardiac surgery-specific decision-making and team-based training protocol was developed by Dunning and colleagues and endorsed by both The Society of Thoracic Surgeons and the European Association of Cardio-Thoracic Surgery (see "Postoperative Cardiac Arrest" in Section I). Maccaroni and colleagues found a decrease in resternotomy rates and an increase in postarrest survival after implementing the protocol. A similar study also found a significant decrease (from 53% to 13%) in failure-to-rescue rates after cardiac arrest. However, both studies are small single-center studies; larger studies are required to conclusively show an association between protocol implementation and a reduction in failure-to-rescue rates.

In summary, a combination of patient and hospital factors contribute to perioperative deaths. Recently developed protocols and recommendations target many of these seminal, potentially death-triggering events to improve patient outcomes after cardiac surgery. However, the literature on the efficacy of these quality improvement initiatives remains sparse. Larger future studies with multi-institutional data sharing will be key to facilitate ongoing knowledge translation in this field.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
###### Summary Points</summary><div class="details-content">

- The cardiac team should consider incorporating the perioperative interdisciplinary team in the "heart team" model of care.
- Modern quality improvement methodology, including phase-of-care mortality analysis and failure-to-rescue analysis, has the potential to significantly improve outcomes after cardiac surgery.
- Preliminary studies indicate that using ERPs to optimize the preoperative, intraoperative, and postoperative phases has improved patient-centered outcomes in patients undergoing cardiac surgery.
- Developing ERPs necessarily requires interdisciplinary collaboration (including patient input) that incorporates evidence-based best practices nested in a local culture of patient-centered care.
- The perioperative team should include essential elements for data reporting in an ERP, to inform quality improvement efforts and facilitate accountability.
- Larger future studies with multi-institutional data sharing are needed to facilitate ongoing quality improvement and knowledge translation in this field.

</div></details>

</div></details>

</div></details>
  
</div></details>

---

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
## SECTION IV: SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS IN THE POSTOPERATIVE CARE OF NEONATES AND INFANTS</summary><div class="details-content">

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
#### General Comments</summary><div class="details-content">

Neonates, infants, and some small young children present postoperative care problems that differ in some ways from those of adults. Principles of subsystems and overall care remain the same, but the internal milieu of even normal neonates and infants is different in certain respects from adults. The cardiac operation has generally been more urgently indicated, and therefore, these young patients often have more preoperative derangements and require more extensive operations. Cardiac reserve of neonates and infants is often less than that of adults after cardiac surgery because of the nature and seriousness of their preoperative cardiac condition. Their small airways are more apt to be suddenly obstructed by secretions, and they are subject to special problems such as episodes of pulmonary arterial hypertensive crises. Serum potassium level rises much more rapidly during oliguria than it does in older children and adults. These considerations do not necessarily indicate that young age per se is a risk factor for death after cardiac surgery; they do indicate that special attention must be given to them. As in Section III of this chapter, we provide the general aspects of postoperative care of neonates and infants here, with details presented in the discussions of the various subsystems.

The endotracheal tube, a urinary catheter, intracardiac recording devices, epicardial myocardial wires, and intravenous access to large central veins are usually in place when the patient leaves the OR, just as in adults. Intravenous access in the caval or internal jugular veins is generally avoided in patients having cavopulmonary connections because of the risk of thrombosis. In the ICU, the patient is placed on a ventilator specifically designed for small patients. Often, low-dose inotropic support, (e.g., dopamine 2.5 to 5 μg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹), is used, even in patients convalescing normally, and this is generally continued until the morning of postoperative day 1.

Because measuring devices and infusion of medications require some fluid, and because it is desirable to limit the total fluid intake in neonates and infants just as it is in adults, all continuously infused medications are mixed in sufficient concentration that the infusion rate does not exceed 2 to 3 mL·h⁻¹, the minimal rate at which reasonably accurate infusions can be given. No "calculated fluids" are planned for the day of operation, and this can be reconsidered early on the morning of postoperative day 1. Calculations are made as to the amount of fluid administered not only by the intravenous route but also in flushing pressure-measuring devices, and at 7 AM of postoperative day 1, consideration is given to beginning administration of "calculated fluids" or continuing without them.

Once the patient is settled on the ventilator, the status of anesthetic and sedating medications is assessed. The goal after complex operations in general is to keep the patient heavily sedated and paralyzed at least until the morning of postoperative day 1. For paralysis, appropriate doses of pancuronium (or a comparable agent) are administered intravenously. This is insufficient to overcome many possibly deleterious reflexes. Therefore, fentanyl is also given as a continuous infusion of about 10 μg·kg⁻¹·h⁻¹, and at times at the increased dose of 15 to 25 μg·kg⁻¹·h⁻¹, not only for sedation but also for control of sympathetic activity. Particularly when the endotracheal tube is suctioned, the patient must be under the influence of fentanyl. Ventilation is arranged to keep arterial Paco₂ at about 30 mmHg, arterial Pao₂ above 120 mmHg when a biventricular repair has been accomplished, and if possible, pH at 7.45 or higher. This minimizes the tendency for pulmonary vascular resistance to rise paroxysmally.

We recommend irrigating the endotracheal tube with saline and suctioning every 2 hours, although some who do not use fentanyl favor no suctioning. Whatever the policy, it must be recognized that this is a potentially hazardous procedure, and it should be undertaken only by skilled nurses with adequate backup, should an emergency develop during or within a few minutes after suctioning.

Early on the morning of postoperative day 1, consideration is given to extubating the patient. If all subsystems appear to be functioning satisfactorily, the protocol leading to extubation is begun. Then, if the patient is awake and active, extubation is accomplished. After extubation, the baby is maintained in a humidified oxygen-enriched environment for at least another 24 hours in the ICU, although care is taken to avoid excessively high Pao₂.

If all subsystems are not functioning well on the morning after operation, there should be no hesitancy to continue control of ventilation as well as paralysis and sedation for another 24 hours. However, about 70% of normally convalescing neonates and small infants can be extubated the morning after operation and the remainder usually on the morning of postoperative day 2.

Following extubation and the passage of a few hours, oral feedings can be started. For the feedings, the patient is held at least in a semi-upright position, observing the usual precautions necessary in feeding small babies. Neonates and young infants have little nutritional reserve. When oral intake is not possible on postoperative day 1 or 2, nasogastric tube feeding is begun. If this is not promptly effective, intravenous alimentation is considered.

To avoid bleeding after their removal, intracardiac catheters should *not* be removed before postoperative day 2. It is prudent to leave all chest tubes/Blake drains in place until after intracardiac lines have been removed.

When convalescence is not normal for any reason, management is altered appropriately, generally as described in the earlier parts of this chapter. A special situation is presented early postoperatively by neonates and young infants when the operation leaves pulmonary blood flow coming solely from a surgically created systemic-pulmonary artery shunt (see Special Features of Postoperative Care in Chapters 34 and 52). In these intubated patients, the magnitude of pulmonary blood flow is largely determined by the relation of systemic to pulmonary vascular resistance. When pulmonary resistance is low, pulmonary blood flow tends to be excessive and systemic blood flow small. Should pulmonary vascular resistance gradually or suddenly rise, pulmonary blood flow declines, sometimes rapidly, and hypoxia develops. In this setting, pulmonary vascular resistance is largely determined by arterial Paco₂ and arterial pH. Manipulation of Paco₂ is the best method for regulating pulmonary vascular resistance. When pulmonary blood flow appears to be too high and systemic blood flow too low, arterial Paco₂ is deliberately elevated modestly (to 40 to 45 mmHg) to increase pulmonary vascular resistance, decrease pulmonary blood flow, and increase systemic blood flow. When pulmonary blood flow seems too low and hypoxia too prominent, pulmonary arteriolar resistance is lowered by lowering the arterial Paco₂ appropriately, usually to 25 to 35 mmHg. Alterations in arterial Paco₂ are conveniently accomplished by altering tidal volume and respiratory rate. The adjustments required are usually sufficiently small that they produce no change in other important variables.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
#### Pulmonary Hypertensive Crises</summary><div class="details-content">

Pulmonary hypertensive crisis is a phrase used to describe a serious syndrome of hyperacute rise in pulmonary arterial pressure, usually accompanied by bronchospasm, often followed within seconds or accompanied by profound reduction in cardiac output and fall in SaO₂; these sequelae may be irreversible. The syndrome occurs most commonly, but not exclusively, among neonates and infants who are intubated after an operation for repair of a congenital cardiac defect associated with pulmonary arterial hypertension. Pulmonary artery pressure is usually essentially normal or mildly elevated after repair, until an episode of pulmonary hypertensive crisis. The crisis may appear spontaneously, but usually occurs during or shortly after endotracheal tube suctioning, particularly if the endotracheal tube is too far down the trachea and makes contact with the carina. Multiple crises may occur in a single patient. The prevalence of pulmonary hypertensive crises is greatest about 18 hours after operation, but they can occur before or after that time. During an acute hypertensive crisis, atypical cardiac tamponade can result from acute RV dilation.

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
##### Incremental Risk Factors</summary><div class="details-content">

Although recognized for many years, its precise cause at the time it occurs is unknown. Congenital cardiac defects accompanied by large pulmonary blood flow and relatively low pulmonary vascular resistance are strong patient risk factors for their development. Chief among them are truncus arteriosus and AV septal defect. The histology of the pulmonary arteries and arterioles is normal or mildly abnormal, with all changes considered clearly reversible. The syndrome is not seen in patients with fixed pulmonary hypertension or severe pulmonary vascular disease. Procedural risk factors have not been clearly identified, although absence of pulmonary blood flow during CPB, accumulation of neutrophils in the lung, and damage to pulmonary endothelial cells are known to occur and provide a favorable setting for development of pulmonary hypertensive crises. Release of arachidonic metabolites from some cells in the lung (probably pulmonary endothelial cells), failure of pulmonary endothelial cells to inactivate bradykinin, and release by injured pulmonary endothelial cells of the vasoconstrictive platelet-activating factor are probably all encouraged by events during CPB, perhaps particularly if these cells were preconditioned before operation by a high pulmonary blood flow. Postoperative risk factors nearly certainly include acute hypoxia, which is known to produce acute hypoxic vasoconstriction, probably using some of the mechanisms just described. Catecholamine administration appears to predispose patients to these crises.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
##### Prevention and Treatment</summary><div class="details-content">

Because pulmonary hypertensive crisis may be fatal despite intense therapy, its prevention is critical. An important preventive measure in neonates and infants with high-risk conditions is maintenance of paralysis and sedation (with pancuronium [or a similar agent] and fentanyl) for at least the first 20 to 24 postoperative hours, and for at least another 24 hours or longer if the patient remains intubated. Fentanyl has been shown to be a safe agent in neonates and adults, with little adverse effect on cardiac function or systemic and pulmonary circulations. A paralytic agent is given along with fentanyl to prevent rigidity. Fentanyl (in a dose of 25 μg·kg⁻¹), along with pancuronium, should be given before suctioning the endotracheal tube in any susceptible neonate or infant who is not already well-paralyzed and sedated because it appears to minimize the possibility of a pulmonary hypertensive crisis. In addition to sedation, aggressive treatment of acidosis, including hyperventilation to Pco₂ = 30 to 34 mmHg, decreases prevalence and severity of pulmonary hypertensive crises. When the monitored pulmonary artery pressure becomes paroxysmally elevated early postoperatively, efforts are made to control and reduce it. A high Fio₂ is maintained because this is as useful an agent as exists for reducing pulmonary vascular resistance.

There are encouraging results from use of inhaled nitric oxide to reduce pulmonary artery pressure in cardiac transplant recipients, patients on LV assist devices, and generally in infants and children prone to pulmonary hypertensive crises. Journis and colleagues found that 15 of 17 children having critical pulmonary hypertension following operation for congenital heart disease responded to inhaled nitric oxide (20 ppm) after failure of conventional medical treatment. Pulmonary arterial pressure decreased without a change in systemic arterial pressure but with concomitant increase of Sao₂ and SVO₂. Other investigators have also shown an increase in cardiac index and improvement of indexes of RV function after nitric oxide treatment. Intrapulmonary shunt fraction may decrease as well. The response in postoperative patients with valvar heart disease may be less satisfactory.

</div></details>

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
#### Junctional Ectopic Tachycardia (JET)</summary><div class="details-content">

JET may be a particularly difficult problem in children, with a prevalence of 5% to 8%. It is associated with operations to close VSDs, perioperative transient AV block, repair of AV septal defect, and young age. The appearance of JET in the postoperative period may be associated with residual lesions, which should be ruled out. In 70 of 71 patients with postoperative JET, Walsh and colleagues obtained control of the arrhythmia and hemodynamic stability using a combination of sedation, hypothermia to 34°C, and procainamide. Amiodarone may also be effective.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
#### Optimal Recovery following Infant Pediatric Cardiac Surgery</summary><div class="details-content">

Recently, there has been an increased focus on optimization of the postoperative evolution of neonates and infants following cardiac surgery. The American Association for Thoracic Surgery has published an expert consensus document on that topic. In the perioperative period, several important elements need to be assessed, including the quality of repair, blood product management, pain management, and respiratory management, among others. The heterogeneity of cardiac malformations, surgical repairs, patients' age and weight, preoperative status, and presence or absence of syndromes makes the standardization of postoperative care challenging. In the end, each patient's care should be individualized within a structured protocol-driven platform.

</div></details>

</div></details>

---

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
## APPENDICES</summary><div class="details-content">

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4A: Protocol for Reducing Arterial Blood Pressure and Afterload</summary><div class="details-content">

**Sodium nitroprusside:** Sodium nitroprusside is continuously administered via IV as required. It acts directly on arterial (and to a lesser extent, venous) smooth muscle and thus decreases systemic vascular resistance, PVR, and systemic venous tone. Its onset and duration of action are immediate. The dose is 1 to 5 µg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹ (doses larger than this are *not* used), regulated in most cases to maintain a mean arterial blood pressure 10% above the normal value for the patient's age. In patients with thick left ventricular walls or coronary artery disease, concern about coronary perfusion pressure makes a mean arterial blood pressure 20% above normal desirable (or 150 mmHg in adults). Twenty-five to 50 mg of sodium nitroprusside are dissolved in 100 to 250 mL of 5% glucose in water; however, this may vary from institution to institution. The drug may be administered with a servo-pump using a closed-loop system under computer control or with a slow-infusion pump. When nitroprusside is being administered, methods should be available for measuring blood thiocyanate levels, because values of >10 g·dL⁻¹ are potentially toxic. Toxicity is manifested by signs of intracellular suppression of oxygen consumption (elevation of mixed venous oxygen levels, narrowing of arterial/venous oxygen difference, and metabolic acidosis) and by anorexia, muscular spasms, disorientation, and convulsions. Toxicity is due largely to the formation of cyanide, the major metabolic product of sodium nitroprusside. Toxicity is treated by stopping the infusion and administration of the hydroxocobalamin (in a cyanide antidote kit) or less commonly an IV infusion over 15 minutes of 150 µg·kg⁻¹ of a 25% solution of sodium thiosulfate (10 µg·kg⁻¹·min⁻¹).

**Nitroglycerin:** Nitroglycerin decreases both venous tone and coronary resistance. It is therefore particularly useful when myocardial ischemia is present. Infusion rates of 5 to 200 µg/min titrated to desired clinical response is recommended. Because nitroglycerin is absorbed into polyvinyl tubing used for IV infusion, the concentration reaching the patient will be less than planned until the tubing becomes saturated. Nitroglycerin is not as effective as nitroprusside in lowering arterial pressure.

**Clevidipine:** Clevidipine is a dihydropyridine L-type calcium-channel blocker that acts on arterial smooth muscle. It is indicated for blood pressure reduction when oral therapy is not feasible or not desirable. The half-life of clevidipine is approximately 1 minute, and the agent is metabolized by esterase in blood to an inactive metabolite and not affected by hepatic or renal function. The initial IV infusion is usually commenced at 1 to 2 mg/h. The dose can be bidirectionally titrated by ≤50% every 90 seconds to 10 minutes. When approaching the desired blood pressure goal, the frequency of dose adjustment can be lengthened to every 5 to 10 minutes to avoid blood pressure target overshoot. A 1 to 2 mg/h increase will generally produce an additional 2 to 4 mmHg decrease in systolic pressure. The maintenance dose is usually 4 to 6 mg/h, with a maximum dose of 16 mg/h up to 72 hours.

Adverse effects of clevidipine include risks for hypotension and reflex tachycardia; headache, nausea, and vomiting also have been reported. Key contraindications to use include allergy to soybeans, soy products, eggs, or egg products and a defective lipid metabolism, given that the vehicle in which the agent is stored contains phospholipids, which can support microbial growth; a good aseptic technique is needed. Lastly, in light of the potential lipid load, a maximum of 1000 mL/24 hours is recommended. The agent contains 2 kcal/mL from fat, which should be counted as a caloric source.

<details class="med-details"><summary>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4B: Standard Infusion Rates of Inotropic and Vasopressor Agents</summary><div class="details-content">

- Dopamine: 1–20 μg/kg/min
- Dobutamine: 1–20 μg/kg/min
- Epinephrine: 0.01–0.2 μg/kg/min
- Norepinephrine: 0.01–1 μg/kg/min
- Milrinone: 0.25–0.75 μg/kg/min
- Vasopressin: 0.01–0.06 units/min
- Angiotensin II: 10–40 ng/kg/min

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4C: Protocol for Managing Aspects of Ventricular Electrical Instability</summary><div class="details-content">

**Early Interventions for Ventricular Electrical Instability**

- 1. Give lidocaine as an IV bolus injection (the dose is 1 mg/kg for adults and children, although in adults the usual dose is 50–100 mg) if the arrhythmia is a PVC or ventricular tachycardia with a good hemodynamic state. If there is ventricular tachycardia and reduction of cardiac output, use *immediate synchronized DC cardioversion* (100 J and then 200 J).
- 2. The perioperative team should seek to maintain the appropriate levels of electrolytes with replacement based on each specific institution.
- 3. If the ventricular rate is <80 to 90 beats/min, initiate pacing. If basic rhythm is sinus or AV junctional, use atrial pacing.

When cardiac rhythm is other than sinus or AV junctional, or atrial pacing fails to result in 1:1 AV conduction, use ventricular pacing (with the ventricular wire electrode attached to negative pole of pacer). In the presence of second- or third-degree AV block, consider AV sequential pacing.

**Intervention after Control of the Urgent Situation**

If the arrhythmia recurs promptly or is not controlled by these simple measures, begin continuous IV lidocaine infusion of 1 to 4 mg/min titrated based on serum lidocaine concentration levels and patient clinical response.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4D: Protocols for Acute Management of Postoperative Atrial Fibrillation</summary><div class="details-content">

The treatment of postoperative atrial fibrillation (POAF) can be approached with either a rate-control or a rhythm-control strategy. Gillinov and colleagues found no significant superiority for either approach in clinical outcomes such as hospitalization length of stay, complication rates, and rates of persistent atrial fibrillation at 60 days.

In hemodynamically stable adult patients, the most commonly used first-line agents are beta-blockers for rate control and amiodarone for rhythm control. Other agents such as verapamil, diltiazem, and digoxin are less commonly used as first-line agents but can be considered if the patient is intolerant or has a contraindication to the usual first-line agents.

**Rate Control Methods**
- Metoprolol 5 mg IV every 3 to 5 minutes (total 15 mg) and/or initiate and titrate oral beta-blockade administration.
- Diltiazem 0.25 mg/kg IV bolus ×1, followed by diltiazem infusion 5 to 15 mg/h titrated to heart rate <100 and with medication hold parameters for a heart rate <60 or a systolic blood pressure <100.
- Digoxin 0.5 mg IV ×1, followed by 0.25 mg IV every 8 hours ×2 doses. In the older adult (>65 years of age), lower body weight or in the presence of compromised renal function, the therapeutic window is narrow, and toxicity may occur. Further, if co-administered with amiodarone, digoxin concentrations will increase so lower dosing will be required and monitored.

**Rhythm Control Methods**
- Amiodarone 150 mg IV loading dose over 20 to 30 minutes, followed by 1 mg/min for 6 hours, then 0.5 mg/min for 18 hours. Transition to amiodarone 200 mg PO BID ×5 day, then 200 mg PO daily. Hold agent if the patient becomes bradycardic (i.e., heart rate <60) or has a prolonged QTc >450. Target a total amiodarone load of 10 g.
- In the hemodynamically unstable patient, managing the patient's airway, breathing, and circulation should be prioritized with plans to undertake timely direct DC cardioversion.

**Anticoagulation**

The prevalence of stroke attributable to postoperative AF is unknown. Although guidelines recommend that anticoagulation be initiated for POAF, the optimal duration of paroxysmal AF that triggers this therapy is a subject of controversy. The justification to initiate anticoagulation if AF persists longer than 48 hours or is recurrent should be balanced between the patient's risk for thromboembolic complications versus the risk for postoperative bleeding. Further, the role of novel oral anticoagulants (e.g., apixaban) versus warfarin remains controversial at the time of this writing. If anticoagulation is being considered:

- **Warfarin with a target INR of 2–3:** It is important to consider the interaction between warfarin bioavailability in patients concurrently being managed with amiodarone and the use of UFH infusion (with titration to a goal activated PTT 2 to 3 times normal or anti-Xa level per the institution's nomogram) as a bridge in appropriate higher-risk patients.
- **Apixaban 5 mg BID for patients with nonvalvular atrial fibrillation:** If the patient meets two of three mitigating criteria (age ≥80, weight ≤60 kg, creatinine ≥1.5 g/dL), the dose may need to be reduced to 2.5 mg.
- Alternative: rivaroxaban 20 mg daily, dose adjusted based on renal function (starting when creatinine clearance (CrCL) <50 mL/min).
- If elective cardioversion is planned, IV heparin is recommended and is prescribed in therapeutic doses.
- If the patient is on dual antiplatelet therapy, strongly consider holding one medication if triple-therapy bleeding risk exceeds benefit.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4E: Protocol for Rapid Atrial Pacing with Atrial Wires</summary><div class="details-content">

The technique of rapid atrial pacing is applied to *atrial flutter*, defined as a general atrial rate of 250 to 350 beats/min with a constant beat-to-beat cycle length that *can be* interrupted by rapid atrial pacing. This is rarely possible in *atrial flutter-fibrillation*, a more rapid type of atrial flutter with a rate >350 beats/min, and it is not possible in AF.

ECG limb leads are placed on the patient for monitoring, and the two atrial wires are connected to the rapid atrial pacer. Because the atrial pacing threshold is usually high during atrial flutter, output is set at 10 to 20 mA. Bipolar atrial pacing is used because the stimulus artifact then rarely distorts the ECG tracing, and the atrial complex in the ECG is clearly seen so that atrial capture can be verified.

A relatively slow pacing rate is used first, because occasionally, immediate one-to-one conduction occurs with rapid ventricular tachycardia, which is to be avoided. After this, several possible maneuvers are available for controlling atrial flutter and its rapid ventricular response, as described by Waldo and MacLean.

**Ramp Technique**

Atrial pacing is initiated at a rate 10 beats/min faster than the atrial flutter rate. The rate of atrial pacing is then gradually increased. When the typical negative atrial complex in lead II changes to a positive atrial complex, indicating capture by pacing, atrial pacing is either abruptly stopped or gradually slowed until the ventricular rate is considered satisfactory.

**Constant Rate Technique**

Pacing is initiated at a rate 10 beats/min faster than the spontaneous atrial flutter rate. After pacing at this rate for about 30 seconds, pacing is either abruptly stopped or the pacing rate quickly slowed until the ventricular rate is considered satisfactory. If these maneuvers are unsuccessful in interrupting the atrial flutter, they are repeated, with the initial atrial pacing rate increased in increments of 10 beats/min.

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4F: Protocol for Initial Mechanical Ventilation Management and Early Extubation</summary><div class="details-content">

This section is an amalgamation of protocols and checklists from the cardiac surgery section and cardiac surgery ICU from St. Boniface Hospital (Winnipeg, Manitoba, Canada) and University Hospitals (Cleveland, Ohio, US).

**Checklist for Extubation**

*Respiratory*
- pH 7.35-7.45
- pco₂ 35–45 mmHg
- Po₂ ≥80 mmHg or SPo₂ ≥95%
- Hemoglobin ≥7.5
- Fio₂ ≤0.5
- Ve <10 L/min
- Able to cough spontaneously
- Minimal respiratory secretions

*Hemodynamics*
- HR 50–110 (may be paced)
- MAP 60–110 mmHg (may be on stable dose of inotrope/vasopressor)
- Chest tube losses <100 mL/h
- Hgb >7.5

*General*
- Temperature >35.5°C
- Pain control adequate
- Able to lift head off pillow for 5 sec
- Conscious and responding to commands
- Able to cough

*Special Considerations*
- Was patient difficult to intubate? Yes / No
- Does patient currently have significant facial or oropharyngeal edema? Yes / No

**If Yes appears for any of the earlier special considerations, a patient may be weaned by the protocol, but a physician order must be obtained to extubate, and a physician must be present for the extubation.**

Was a physician presence needed? Yes / No

Time extubated: _______________

Form completed by: _______________

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4G: Protocol for Acute Kidney Injury Risk Assessment and Prevention</summary><div class="details-content">

This protocol is adapted with permission from the Harrington Heart and Vascular Institute, University Hospitals (Cleveland, Ohio, US) AKI Prevention protocol.

**Step 1: Preoperative Evaluation and Education**

Patients at risk for cardiac surgery-associated acute kidney injury AKI can be identified preoperatively by using a dynamic predictive scoring tool (such as that adapted from Jiang and colleagues shown later). Patients undergoing elective cardiac surgery should be assigned to a low, moderate, or high-risk category based on a scoring tool used in perioperative medicine. The patient's risk for perioperative AKI should be documented in the patient's chart as part of the preoperative history and physical (H&P). Patients should continue to receive preoperative cardiac surgery education with emphasis on holding all NSAIDs at least 1 week before surgery. Angiotensin-converting enzyme and angiotensin receptor blocker agents (ACE/ARBs) should be discontinued at least 48 hours preoperatively.

| Variables | Points |
|---|---|
| Male | 1 |
| Age: <40 y | 0 |
| Age: 41-60 y | 1 |
| Age: 61-80 y | 2 |
| Age: >81 y | 3 |
| Chronic kidney disease | 1 |
| New York Heart Association score >2 | 1 |
| Previous cardiac surgery | 1 |

**Step 2: Intraoperative Identification of Risk Factors and Patient Care Considerations**

The patient's AKI risk score will be discussed during the intraoperative pre-incision huddle and added to any intraoperative checklist. Intraoperative hemodynamic monitoring and fluid management will continue per cardiac anesthesia discretion, keeping the patient's risk status in mind. High-risk patients may receive a pulmonary artery catheter or other minimally invasive cardiac output-monitoring device. Anesthesia should ensure preoperative methicillin-resistant *Staphylococcus aureus* (MRSA) swab results are reviewed; intraoperative vancomycin should be avoided if possible.

**Step 3: Postoperative Assessment of AKI Risk Factor**

The receiving team in the ICU should review the patient's preoperative risk score during the OR-to-ICU handover. The ICU provider will then calculate a new risk score based on the dynamic predictive scoring tool at the time of ICU admittance, as shown here. The patient's postoperative risk score should be documented within the initial ICU H&P and incorporated into the genitourinary section of the patient's daily assessment and plan in the progress note. If vancomycin was administered in the OR, the resident or advanced practice provider should review MRSA swab status and send swab if necessary. Only order vancomycin postoperatively if the patient is MRSA positive or if swab results are still pending.

| Variables | Points |
|---|---|
| Male | 1 |
| Age: <40 y | 0 |
| Age: 41-60 y | 1 |
| Age: 61-80 y | 2 |
| Age: >81 y | 3 |
| Chronic kidney disease | 1 |
| New York Heart Association score >2 | 1 |
| Previous cardiac surgery | 1 |
| Valve and coronary artery bypass grafting | 1 |
| Erythrocyte transfusion (per unit) | 1/unit |

**Step 4: Implementation of Goal-Directed Therapies for High-Risk Patients**

All patients at high risk for AKI should be treated according to a postoperative hemodynamic algorithm like the one later. Overall goals for high-risk patients should include a mean arterial pressure (MAP) of 75 to 90 mmHg, a CVP of 10 to 12 mmHg, and a cardiac index (CI) >2.5 L/min/m². A postoperative ICU cardiac surgery intensive insulin infusion order set should be used for all high-risk patients during the first 12 to 24 hours to allow for optimal blood glucose management and to avoid hyperglycemia, defined as >180 mg/dL for more than 3 hours. No ACE/ARB medications or NSAIDs should be given to high-risk patients for at least 48 hours postoperatively. Avoid radiocontrast agents for at least 48 hours postoperatively in high-risk patients. No hydroxyethyl starch, gelatin, or chloride-rich solutions; first-choice crystalloid fluid should be lactated Ringer's or Plasmalyte. Use normal saline if patient has, or is at risk for developing, clinically significant hyperkalemia.

(Image: Hemodynamic algorithm diagram)

**Step 5: Ongoing ICU Evaluation and Monitoring**

The renal function panel should be monitored at least every 12 hours during the first 24 hours for all elective cardiac surgery cases, regardless of risk score. For all elective cardiac surgery patients, AKI risk should be reassessed at the 24-hour mark by using the dynamic predictive scoring tool, as shown here.

| Variables | Points |
|---|---|
| Male | 1 |
| Age: <40 y | 0 |
| Age: 41-60 y | 1 |
| Age: 61-80 y | 2 |
| Age: >81 y | 3 |
| Chronic kidney disease | 1 |
| Cardiopulmonary bypass application | 1 |
| Previous cardiac surgery | 1 |
| Valve and coronary artery bypass grafting | 1 |
| Erythrocyte transfusion (per unit) | 1/unit |
| Low cardiac output syndrome | 1 |

**Step 6: Outcome Measurement and Monitoring**

Outcomes will be reported and monitored as a component of ongoing quality efforts, including participation in STS registries. Use of the dynamic predictive scoring tool will be tracked through chart reviews of H&Ps and daily progress notes.

**Risk Stratification of CSA-AKI Development at Indicated Time Points**

| Sum Points | Preoperative | ICU Admittance | 24 Hours after ICU Admittance |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0–1 pts | Low | Low | Low |
| 2–3 pts | Medium | Low | Low |
| 4 pts | High | Low | Low |
| 5–9 pts | High | Medium | Medium |
| >10 pts | High | High | High |

</div></details>

<details class="med-details"><summary>
  
### 4H: Example of an ERAS Implementation Checklist</summary><div class="details-content">

Adapted from Salenger et al.

**ENHANCED RECOVERY AFTER SURGERY IMPLEMENTATION CHECKLIST**

**A. Creating a case for change**
- 1. Gap analysis of a service line
  - a. Follow patient through the surgical journey
  - b. Process/value stream mapping
  - c. Teamwork: determine communication gaps between providers in all phases of care □
- 2. Create vision of future pathways
  - a. How will the interdisciplinary team work in delivering pathways?
  - b. Look at the evidence and decide which elements to incorporate
  - c. Obtain patient feedback on their experience of surgical case
  - d. Identify clinical stakeholders necessary to make the change
  - e. Recognize what is going well and what needs to change □
- 3. Collect baseline data of service line:
  - a. Process measures
  - b. Existing protocols
  - c. Outcome measures
  - d. Existing dashboards/STS outcomes

**B. Communicate the case for change**
- 1. Done by executive sponsor and clinical lead (champion) □
- 2. Communicate all information collected during Step #1
- 3. Communicate to all internal clinical areas
- 4. Communication to be consistent
- 5. Address any and all questions/pushback
- 6. Clinical Change: All clinical change is vetted through the ERAS Committee with opportunity for input and approval from key stakeholders

**C. Building the ERAS team: Include name and contact information**
- 1. Surgeon Champion(s) □
- 2. Anesthesiologist Champion(s) □
- 3. Nurse Champion(s)/ERAS Coordinator □
- 4. Hospital Administration □
- 5. CRNA(s) □
- 6. Service Line Navigator □
- 7. Pharmacist □
- 8. Allied Staff: Physical Therapist, Occupational Therapist, Respiratory Therapist, CAN, Exercise Tech, Nutritionist □
- 9. Administrative support staff □
- 10. Case Manager □
- 11. Quality Improvement □
- 12. Dimensions □
- 13. Capital Equipment Committee member □
- 14. Other □

**D. Clarify ERAS Committee Roles, Responsibilities, and Goals**
- 1. Goals Established □
- 2. ERAS Committee Meeting Schedule Established
  - **who owns the meeting invite and agenda development? □
- 3. Committee Chair(s) □
- 4. Protocol Development Team established
  - **set deadline for protocol completion □
- 5. Patient Education Materials Team established
  - **set deadline for patient education materials □
- 6. Nursing Education Team established □
- 7. Order Set Development Team established □
- 8. ERAS Data Collection Team established □
- 9. Capital Needs Assessment: Complete and submit EARLY, should be minimal □

**E. Education Planning: Plan to begin when protocol nears finalization**
- 1. All care settings (mandatory: in-person education sessions)
  - *include APP, Residents, RN, CNA, PT, OT, RT, Exercise Tech, Rehab, Pharmacist, Case Manager □
  - • Office
  - • Preop Clinic
  - • Preop holding
  - • PACU
  - • OR
  - • ICU
  - • Floor
- 2. Anesthesiologist/CRNA education □

**F. Patient Education Materials**
- 1. Begin early in process and finalize after program finalization □
- 2. Considerations □
  - a. Hospital process to approve patient materials
  - b. Patient input during development
  - c. Different learning styles
  - d. Ability to modify materials in future

**G. Questions to Consider**
- 1. All patients or a subset? □
- 2. Who will provide patient education and where? □
- 3. Will message always be the same? □

**H. Create ERAS dashboard/database**
- 1. Determine who will collect and report the data □
- 2. Process Metrics □
- 3. Outcomes Dashboard □

**I. Other Completion Items**
- 1. Protocol Checklists in ORs (in binders or on computer desktop) □
- 2. Protocols/Checklists in central location or EHR □

**J. Implementation Day**
- 1. Have patient sign marketing release (possible story on hospital intranet) □
- 2. Make sure all MD orders are complete and correct □
- 3. Designated person in care areas, following patient, available for questions/help as needed □
- 4. Celebrate □

**K. Future Planning**
- 1. Review outcome and process data – Key! □
- 2. Report data to ERAS Committee and disseminate to all care areas □
- 3. If low compliance, address with nursing leadership AND ask front line staff challenges □
- 4. Review PDSA □
- 5. Plan for expansion of program/further innovation □

</div></details>
